# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 273

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Rand, D. G., Dreber, A., Ellingsen, T., Fudenberg, D., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Positive Interactions Promote Public Cooperation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games with fixed groups, both punishment and reward mechanisms can maintain high levels of cooperation and efficiency compared to a control with no targeted interactions. However, reward leads to higher total payoffs than punishment, as punishment is costly and does not increase efficiency beyond what is achieved by reward. When both reward and punishment are available, groups that use reward more achieve higher payoffs, while groups that use punishment more achieve lower payoffs. The results suggest that positive reciprocity (reward) is more effective than negative reciprocity (punishment) for promoting efficiency in repeated public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games with persistent group identity and known group membership, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to a control, but enabling reward is equally or more effective and leads to higher group payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment alone should be expected to increase efficiency compared to no-punishment controls, but not as much as reward or combined reward/punishment treatments. The effect of punishment is less positive for efficiency than reward, and when both are available, punishment may even reduce efficiency due to its cost. The findings are most applicable to repeated, fixed-group, non-anonymous settings with continuous contribution choices and no communication.

- source: Sutter, M., Haigner, S., & Kocher, M. G. (2010). Choosing the Carrot or the Stick? Endogenous Institutional Choice in Social Dilemma Situations. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling groups to endogenously choose their institution (via voting) leads to higher contributions and higher group profits (efficiency) than exogenously imposed institutions, holding the institution constant. Both punishment and reward options increase contributions and profits compared to the standard VCM, but the effect is much stronger when the punishment/reward technology is highly effective (high leverage). However, punishment is rarely chosen when groups can choose, likely due to its costliness; groups prefer reward when it is effective. In exogenous treatments, high-leverage reward yields the highest profits, followed by high-leverage punishment, with the standard VCM lowest. The efficiency gains from punishment are offset by the cost of punishing, while reward can be efficiency-enhancing if its leverage is high. The positive effect of endogenous choice is not explained by selection of more cooperative types into certain institutions, but rather by the act of participation itself.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with 4 players and 10 rounds, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness) increases group efficiency (average profit) compared to the standard VCM, but the efficiency gain is limited by the cost of punishment. Reward, when highly effective, yields even higher efficiency than punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive but smaller than the effect on contributions, due to the costliness of punishment. Endogenous institutional choice (voting) further increases efficiency for any given institution. For prediction, the presence and effectiveness of punishment/reward, the cost/impact ratio, and whether the institution is chosen endogenously or exogenously are all important moderators. Control efficiency (standard VCM) is always lower than with effective punishment or reward, but the size of the efficiency gain depends on the punishment/reward technology and institutional choice procedure.

- source: Fehr, E., & Gächter, S. (2000). Cooperation and punishment in public goods experiments. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing a costly punishment option in a repeated public goods game with groups of four, a known 10-round horizon, and no communication or identification, leads to a dramatic increase in average contributions and, after an initial period of adjustment, to higher group efficiency (payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. In the no-punishment condition, contributions and efficiency decline toward full free-riding. In the punishment condition, contributions are sustained at high levels (often near full cooperation), and group payoffs in the final periods are 10-20% higher than in the control. Punishment is used primarily against those who contribute less than the group average, and punished individuals tend to increase their contributions in subsequent rounds. The effect is robust across both stranger (random rematching) and partner (fixed group) treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling costly punishment in a standard linear public goods game with four players, ten rounds, no communication, and anonymous interactions. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low (due to declining contributions), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency by 10-20% in the final rounds, with the effect being stronger in fixed groups (partner) than in random groups (stranger). The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, known horizon, full feedback, anonymous punishment) closely match the canonical prediction task, making this paper highly informative for forecasting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled.

- source: Ambrus, A., & Greiner, B. (2012). Imperfect Public Monitoring with Costly Punishment: An Experimental Study. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In 50-round, three-player public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, the introduction of costly punishment options has different effects depending on monitoring and punishment severity. With perfect monitoring, both regular and strong punishment increase average net payoffs, with stronger punishment yielding higher efficiency. With imperfect (noisy) monitoring, regular punishment reduces efficiency below the no-punishment baseline, while strong punishment increases cooperation but only restores efficiency to the no-punishment level (i.e., the gains from increased cooperation are offset by the costs of punishment). The relationship between punishment severity and efficiency is U-shaped under noise: intermediate punishment is worst for efficiency, while strong punishment is neutral. The paper provides direct, quantitative evidence on efficiency outcomes for each treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, especially as a function of monitoring quality and punishment severity. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive: under imperfect monitoring, standard punishment can reduce efficiency, and only very strong punishment can (at best) restore efficiency to the no-punishment baseline. The results are directly applicable to settings with all-or-nothing contributions, 3 players, 50 rounds, and known MPCR and punishment parameters. The findings highlight the importance of monitoring quality and punishment effectiveness in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gürerk, Irlenbusch, B., & Rockenbach, B. (2006). The competitive advantage of sanctioning institutions. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with endogenous institution choice, participants could choose between a sanctioning institution (with both positive and negative sanctions) and a sanction-free institution. Initially, most participants preferred the sanction-free institution, but over time, nearly all migrated to the sanctioning institution. In the sanctioning institution, cooperation and efficiency increased steadily, reaching over 95% of the maximum possible group payoff in the final period, while the sanction-free institution's efficiency fell to zero. The presence of punishment (negative sanctions) was the key driver of sustained high cooperation and efficiency. The study demonstrates that sanctioning institutions have a strong competitive advantage and can resolve the social dilemma by attracting and converting even initially noncooperative individuals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game dramatically increases efficiency compared to a control condition without punishment, even when participants can freely choose between institutions. The design closely matches canonical PGGs, and the efficiency outcome is explicitly measured. The findings support strong positive predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in repeated games with migration or institution choice. The specific punishment cost and effectiveness parameters, as well as the presence of both positive and negative sanctions, are informative for parameterizing prediction models.

- source: Grechenig, K., Nicklisch, A., & Thöni, C. (2010). Punishment Despite Reasonable Doubt-A Public Goods Experiment with Sanctions Under Uncertainty. *Journal of Empirical Legal Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a standard repeated public goods game with and without punishment, and with varying levels of noise in information about others' contributions, the paper finds that: (1) Punishment increases efficiency and cooperation only when information is accurate; (2) With moderate or high noise, punishment does not improve cooperation and reduces efficiency below the no-punishment baseline; (3) Subjects continue to punish under noise, but punishment is less targeted and more frequent, leading to more antisocial punishment and welfare loss; (4) The efficiency loss from punishment under noise is substantial and persistent, even though subjects could choose not to punish. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment as a cooperation-enforcing mechanism is highly contingent on the accuracy of information about others' behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency benefit of enabling punishment in public goods games depends critically on the accuracy of information about others' contributions. When information is accurate, punishment increases efficiency; when information is noisy, punishment reduces efficiency below the no-punishment baseline. For prediction, this means that the presence of noise or uncertainty in monitoring is a key moderator: if the game design includes noisy or imperfect information about contributions, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency. If information is accurate, punishment is likely to increase efficiency. Other design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are held constant in this study, so the main actionable moderator is information accuracy.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Milinski, M. (2006). The efficient interaction of indirect reciprocity and costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants can choose between groups with or without costly punishment, and with or without an indirect reciprocity (reputation) mechanism, most eventually prefer the group with both punishment and reputation. The combination (PUN&IR) leads to higher contributions, more efficient use of punishment (less frequent, more targeted), and significantly higher efficiency (group payoff as a fraction of the social optimum) than punishment alone (PUN). The destructive effect of punishment on efficiency is largely mitigated by the presence of reputation, as punishment is used less but more effectively. Full contributors in the combined treatment achieve payoffs close to the social optimum, and even the least cooperative subjects are more cooperative in the combined treatment. The results suggest that punishment and reputation are complementary, not substitutes, and their interaction provides a low-cost, high-efficiency solution to social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with costly punishment, adding a reputation/indirect reciprocity mechanism increases efficiency beyond what is achieved by punishment alone. For prediction tasks, the presence of both punishment and reputation should be expected to yield higher efficiency than punishment alone, especially when punishment is costly and group members can observe each other's contributions and reputational history. The efficiency boost is due to reduced punishment frequency, more targeted punishment, and increased cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially when both punishment and reputation mechanisms are present.

- source: Fudenberg, D., & Pathak, P. A. (2010). Unobserved punishment supports cooperation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This experiment compares two versions of a public goods game with punishment: one where punishment is revealed after each round (Observed), and one where punishment is only revealed at the end of 10 rounds (Unobserved). Both treatments use random rematching, groups of 4, 10 rounds per set, and a standard VCM payoff structure (endowment 20, MPCR 0.4). Punishment costs 1 token per point assigned and reduces the target's payoff by 3 tokens per point. The main findings are: (1) average contributions are significantly higher in the Unobserved treatment than in the Observed treatment; (2) average income (efficiency) is also higher in the Unobserved treatment, though confidence intervals overlap in some periods; (3) the fraction of participants who punish and punishment expenditure are at least as high, or higher, in the Unobserved treatment; (4) punishment is more sensitive to undercontribution in the Unobserved treatment. The results show that costly punishment supports cooperation and efficiency even when it cannot have immediate strategic or reputational effects, suggesting an intrinsic motivation for punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with punishment, the presence of a punishment option increases efficiency (average income) relative to the baseline with no punishment, and that this effect is robust to whether punishment is immediately observed or only revealed at the end. The game design dimensions are well specified: 4 players, 10 rounds per set, no chat, continuous contribution, no default, MPCR 0.4, punishment cost 1, punishment tech 3, no reward, random rematching, and full feedback on others' contributions. The results suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, and that the effect is not dependent on immediate feedback about punishment. The paper is directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design dimensions in standard PGGs with punishment.

- source: Nikiforakis, N. (2008). Punishment and counter-punishment in public good games: Can we really govern ourselves?. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) standard public good game (VCM, no punishment), (2) public good game with one-sided punishment (P), and (3) public good game with two-sided punishment/counter-punishment (PCP). In the P treatment, punishment increases contributions but not enough to offset the cost of punishment, so average earnings are lower than in the control. In the PCP treatment, the threat of counter-punishment reduces the willingness to punish free riders, leading to lower contributions and even lower earnings than in both the control and the P treatment. The presence of counter-punishment opportunities causes a breakdown of cooperation and group welfare. The results are robust to matching protocol (fixed vs random) and sequencing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard linear public goods games with 4 players, 10 rounds, no communication, and standard punishment technology, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to the control (no punishment), and enabling counter-punishment reduces efficiency even further. The effect is robust across matching protocols. For prediction, if the control efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, and enabling counter-punishment will reduce it further. The paper also provides detailed parameterization of the punishment cost and technology, which can inform predictions for similar designs.

- source: Gächter, S., & Herrmann, B. (2011). The limits of self-governance when cooperators get punished: Experimental evidence from urban and rural Russia. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to much of the previous literature, introducing a punishment option in one-shot public goods games in Russia did not increase cooperation or group efficiency. In fact, average earnings (a direct measure of efficiency) were substantially lower in the punishment condition than in the no-punishment condition across all subject pools. This is attributed to high levels of punishment directed at cooperators as well as free riders, leading to large payoff losses. The effect is robust across urban/rural and young/mature subject pools. The findings are supported by both within-subject and between-subject comparisons, and are further corroborated by similar results in repeated-game settings in Russia.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot public goods games with a punishment option, the effect of punishment on efficiency can be negative if misdirected punishment is prevalent. The game design here (n=3, one-shot, MPCR=0.5, punishment cost=1, punishment impact=3, no chat, no reward, continuous contributions) is highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment in a similar Russian context is likely to reduce efficiency, not increase it. The findings highlight the importance of considering the prevalence of antisocial punishment and the social context when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Conte, R., Sabater-Mir, J., Solaz, H., & Villatoro, D. (2013). Punish and Voice: Punishment Enhances Cooperation when Combined with Norm-Signalling. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with 4-person groups over 30 rounds, the study compares three treatments: (1) punishment only, (2) norm-signaling (message) only, and (3) both punishment and norm-signaling (sanction). In the critical rounds where treatments differ (rounds 11-20), the sanction treatment yields significantly higher average net earnings (31% higher than punishment, 16% higher than message) and higher contributions, with less frequent and less intense punishment. The efficiency gains in the sanction treatment are attributed to the combination of explicit norm communication and the credible threat of punishment, which together increase norm salience and reduce the need for costly punishment. The findings are robust in both human experiments and agent-based simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with costly punishment, the addition of norm-signaling (explicit communication of normative expectations) to punishment substantially increases group efficiency compared to punishment alone. The efficiency gains are due to both higher cooperation and lower punishment costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of norm-signaling or communication channels that make norms salient can be a critical moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of punishment, the ability to send norm-signaling messages, and the structure of feedback about others' actions. The control efficiency (without punishment or norm-signaling) is lower than in the combined treatment. The results imply that, all else equal, enabling both punishment and norm-signaling will yield higher efficiency than enabling punishment alone.

- source: Masclet, D., Noussair, C., Tucker, S., & Villeval, M. C. (2003). Monetary and nonmonetary punishment in the voluntary contributions mechanism. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing either monetary or nonmonetary punishment in a standard voluntary contributions mechanism (VCM) increases both contributions and group earnings compared to a baseline with no punishment. Nonmonetary (costless, expressive) punishment increases contributions and earnings immediately, while monetary punishment initially reduces earnings due to punishment costs but eventually leads to higher contributions and similar earnings as nonmonetary punishment. The effect of nonmonetary punishment is stronger when groups are stable (Partner matching) than when they are rematched (Stranger matching). Both types of punishment are most effective at increasing contributions among those who contributed less than the group average. The study provides direct evidence that both formal (monetary) and informal (nonmonetary) sanctions can increase efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (either monetary or nonmonetary) in a standard PGG/VCM increases group efficiency (average earnings) compared to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust across two forms of punishment, but monetary punishment is more effective at sustaining high contributions over time, though it incurs costs that initially reduce efficiency. The design dimensions—such as group size (4), number of rounds (10 per segment), no chat, continuous contributions, known MPCR (0.4), and anonymous punishment—are all specified, making the findings directly applicable to prediction tasks involving similar game structures. The results also highlight that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost structure and the matching protocol (Partner vs Stranger), with stable groups supporting more effective informal sanctions. For prediction, the presence of punishment (especially monetary) should be expected to increase efficiency, but the magnitude and time profile may depend on punishment cost and group stability.

- source: Sääksvuori, L., Mappes, T., & Puurtinen, M. (2011). Costly punishment prevails in intergroup conflict. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In isolated public goods games, costly punishment increases cooperation but does not significantly increase net pay-off (efficiency), as the costs of punishment offset the gains from higher contributions. When intergroup competition is introduced, groups with punishment opportunities achieve much higher contributions and net pay-offs, both in asymmetric (one group can punish, one cannot) and symmetric (both can punish) competition. In asymmetric competition, the efficiency benefit is dramatic: even the lowest-earning individual in the punishment group outperforms the highest in the no-punishment group. In symmetric competition, the efficiency benefit is positive but smaller and only marginally significant. Punishment also reduces within-group inequality in pay-offs. The paper concludes that intergroup competition is a key moderator of the efficiency effects of costly punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on whether there is intergroup competition. In the absence of group competition, enabling punishment does not reliably increase efficiency. With group competition, punishment can substantially increase efficiency, especially when only some groups have access to punishment. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence of group competition, punishment cost and effectiveness, group size (8), number of rounds (30), and the absence of communication or reputation mechanisms. The control efficiency (no punishment) is much lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment) in the presence of group competition, but not in isolated groups. This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with and without punishment, especially when intergroup competition is a design feature.

- source: O'Gorman, R., Henrich, J., & Van Vugt, M. (2009). Constraining free riding in public goods games: designated solitary punishers can sustain human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compared three conditions in a repeated public goods game: (1) no punishment (control), (2) all group members can punish (all-punishment), and (3) only one randomly selected group member can punish (one-punishment). Both punishment conditions led to significantly higher and more stable contributions than the control. Profits (efficiency) were higher in the one-punishment condition than in the all-punishment condition, and both were more stable than the control. The cost of punishment reduced profits relative to the control in the short run, but the control condition's profits declined over rounds due to decaying cooperation, while punishment conditions maintained stable profits. The one-punisher regime avoids redundant punishment costs, making it more efficient. The punishment mechanism used a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio. The study provides direct evidence that both diffuse and designated punishment can sustain cooperation and efficiency, but a single punisher can do so more efficiently.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard four-player, six-round public goods game with a 1:3 punishment cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment increases efficiency (group profit) relative to a no-punishment control. Furthermore, a single designated punisher is as effective as allowing all members to punish in sustaining cooperation, but is more efficient due to lower aggregate punishment costs. For prediction, this suggests that, under similar game parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech), enabling punishment will increase efficiency, and that the structure of the punishment regime (single vs. diffuse) can moderate the efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2016). Gossip Versus Punishment: The Efficiency of Reputation to Promote and Maintain Cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally compares the effects of punishment and gossip on cooperation and efficiency in a four-round public goods game. Punishment (fee-to-fine ratio 1:3) did not significantly increase overall cooperation and significantly reduced individual earnings (efficiency) compared to control. Gossip (costless) increased both cooperation and efficiency. When both mechanisms were present, gossip offset the negative efficiency impact of punishment. The study also finds that gossip, but not punishment, has positive spillover effects on trust and trustworthiness in a subsequent trust game. The results suggest that punishment, at moderate cost, is less efficient than gossip for promoting cooperation, and that gossip can mitigate the efficiency losses from punishment.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard four-player, four-round PGG with a punishment mechanism (fee-to-fine ratio 1:3, anonymous, no chat, continuous contributions), enabling punishment reduces efficiency (total/individual earnings) compared to control. The negative effect is robust, and the presence of a costless gossip mechanism can counteract this loss. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar PGG designs, especially when punishment is moderately costly and anonymous. The results also suggest that the efficiency cost of punishment is not offset by increased cooperation, except possibly in the final rounds, and that gossip is a more efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation.

- source: Gross, J., Méder, Z. Z., Okamoto-Barth, S., & Riedl, A. (2016). Building the Leviathan - Voluntary centralisation of punishment power sustains cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study introduces a 'power transfer game'—a repeated public goods game with punishment, where group members can voluntarily transfer their punishment power to others. In groups of five, three conditions were compared: (1) endogenous (voluntary) power transfer, (2) exogenous (mirrored, but not voluntary) power transfer, and (3) fixed (no transfer, standard peer punishment). Only the endogenous condition, where group members could choose whom to empower, led to sustained or increasing cooperation and higher group earnings, approaching the social optimum. In the fixed and exogenous conditions, cooperation and group earnings declined over time. The mechanism works because non-punishing cooperators delegate power to those willing to punish, and centralisation of power in the hands of pro-social punishers deters free riding. The study provides direct evidence that voluntary centralisation of punishment power can efficiently sustain cooperation and group welfare in public goods dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with peer punishment, allowing voluntary centralisation of punishment power (i.e., enabling group members to transfer their punishment effectiveness to others) substantially increases group efficiency compared to standard decentralised peer punishment. The effect is not observed when centralisation is imposed exogenously. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a voluntary power transfer mechanism is a key moderator: with it, efficiency is much higher than in standard peer punishment, even when the underlying MPCR, punishment cost, and other parameters are held constant. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency in games with and without voluntary centralisation of punishment power.

- source: Gächter, S., Kölle, F., & Quercia, S. (2017). Reciprocity and the tragedies of maintaining and providing the commons. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across four lab experiments, the authors compare public goods games where the group must either maintain an existing public good (maintenance) or create one from scratch (provision). Without punishment, cooperation and efficiency are systematically lower in maintenance than provision, despite identical incentives. When a costly punishment option is introduced (partners with punishment), both maintenance and provision games see a dramatic increase in the effective size of the public good, with efficiency rising to near-optimal levels and no significant difference between the two conditions. The punishment mechanism allows each player to assign up to five punishment points to each other, costing 1 MU per point and reducing the target's earnings by 3 MU per point. The paper also finds that negative reciprocity (punishment in response to low contributions) is stronger in maintenance than provision, and that the response to being punished (increased cooperation) is also stronger in maintenance. Overall, punishment is highly effective at restoring efficiency in both types of public goods dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, 27 rounds, and a punishment stage (cost 1 MU, impact 3 MU), the introduction of punishment increases group efficiency from low levels (especially in maintenance) to high levels, nearly matching the social optimum. The effect is robust across both 'maintenance' and 'provision' variants, and the punishment mechanism is highly effective at overcoming the decay in cooperation seen without punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment in similar lab PGG designs will substantially increase efficiency, regardless of whether the dilemma is framed as maintenance or provision. The specific punishment technology (cost/impact) and the absence of communication or rewards are important contextual features.

- source: Drouvelis, M., & Grosskopf, B. (2016). The effects of induced emotions on pro-social behaviour. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study uses a one-shot voluntary contributions mechanism (VCM) public goods game with and without punishment, manipulating both the announcement of punishment and participants' emotional state (anger vs. happiness). The main findings are: (1) Announcing the punishment stage increases contributions and average net earnings, confirming that punishment can improve efficiency in the short run. (2) Induced anger reduces contributions and increases the severity of punishment, leading to lower average net earnings (i.e., lower efficiency) compared to the happy condition. (3) The efficiency benefit of punishment is present but is diminished when subjects are angry, as harsher punishment increases costs and reduces net group payoffs. (4) The effect of punishment on efficiency is measured directly via average net earnings, and the design allows for clean causal inference due to the one-shot structure and between-subjects manipulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a one-shot, three-player VCM with punishment, the introduction of a punishment stage (when announced) increases group efficiency (average net earnings) compared to the same game without punishment. The magnitude of the efficiency gain depends on the emotional state of participants: it is larger when subjects are happy and smaller (but still positive) when subjects are angry, due to increased punishment costs. The game design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, etc.) are all clearly specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the efficiency effect of punishment in similar lab settings. The results suggest that, controlling for baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency, but the effect can be moderated by factors (such as mood) that affect the severity and frequency of punishment.

- source: Dickinson, D. L., & Masclet, D. (2015). Emotion venting and punishment in public good experiments. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a standard 4-player, 10-round public goods game with punishment (costly to punisher, more costly to target), the authors introduce several treatments allowing for different levels of emotion venting before punishment decisions: (1) Baseline (no venting), (2) Waiting (5-min cooling-off), (3) Waiting & Emotion (cooling-off plus self-reported emotions and unsent messages), and (4) Virtual Punishment (all previous plus non-binding virtual punishment). All treatments have punishment enabled. The main findings are: (a) All venting treatments reduce the amount and frequency of costly punishment compared to baseline; (b) The simple cooling-off period (Waiting) increases final payoffs (efficiency) relative to baseline, as reduced excessive punishment outweighs any loss in contributions; (c) More extensive venting (Virtual Punishment) reduces both punishment and contributions, and only increases efficiency in the early rounds, not in the long run; (d) The relationship between emotion venting and efficiency is non-linear (inverted-U): moderate venting is optimal for efficiency, while too little or too much venting is suboptimal. The study does not include a no-punishment control, so all results are relative to punishment-enabled baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard lab PGG with punishment, the efficiency effect of punishment depends on the emotional context of punishment decisions. If punishment is enabled without any venting, excessive punishment can reduce efficiency. Introducing a simple cooling-off period (moderate venting) reduces excessive punishment and increases efficiency. However, if venting is too extensive (e.g., virtual punishment), punishment is reduced too much, contributions fall, and efficiency gains are not sustained. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is maximized when the game design includes a moderate opportunity for emotional cooling-off before punishment, but not when venting is so extensive that it undermines the deterrence function of punishment. The results are most relevant for standard 4-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGGs with punishment enabled and no communication.

- source: Andreoni, J., & Gee, L. K. (2012). Gun for hire: Delegated enforcement and peer punishment in public goods provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated linear public goods games with four players, the introduction of a delegated punishment mechanism (either imposed or endogenously hired) leads to a substantial and immediate increase in both contributions and net earnings (welfare), compared to both the no-punishment baseline and the peer-to-peer punishment regime. When both delegated and peer-to-peer punishment are available, subjects overwhelmingly choose to implement the delegated mechanism, which then crowds out peer punishment and further increases efficiency. The delegated mechanism is low-cost, targets only the largest free rider, and is effective at deterring free riding. The efficiency gains are robust across different baseline conditions (with or without peer punishment as the initial regime).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players and repeated rounds, enabling a delegated punishment mechanism (with a low per-person cost and a punishment that targets only the largest free rider) increases group efficiency (net earnings) substantially compared to both no-punishment and peer-to-peer punishment regimes. The efficiency gains are primarily due to a reduction in costly peer-to-peer punishment, not just increased contributions. The findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar game designs, especially when the punishment is centralized, low-cost, and targets the worst free rider. The results are less directly informative for other forms of punishment (e.g., peer-to-peer only, or high-cost/low-impact punishment), but the contrast with peer-to-peer punishment is explicitly tested and shows delegated punishment is superior for efficiency.

- source: Nikiforakis, N., Noussair, C. N., & Wilkening, T. (2012). Normative conflict and feuds: The limits of self-enforcement. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab public goods game, groups of four played 10 rounds with or without punishment. In the 'Symmetric' treatment (all players have the same marginal per-capita return), punishment opportunities led to higher contributions and significantly higher group earnings (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment baseline. In the 'Asymmetric' treatment (two players have high, two have low returns), punishment also increased contributions, but the frequency and severity of counter-punishment and feuds rose sharply, leading to high punishment costs. As a result, group earnings in the Asymmetric treatment with punishment were not significantly higher than in the no-punishment baseline. The study demonstrates that the efficiency benefits of punishment depend critically on the absence of normative conflict: when such conflict is present, punishment can fail to improve, or even reduce, efficiency due to costly feuding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling punishment in public goods games depends on group homogeneity and the presence of normative conflict. In symmetric (homogeneous) groups, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. In asymmetric (heterogeneous) groups with normative conflict, enabling punishment increases cooperation but not efficiency, as punishment costs (especially from feuds) offset the gains. For prediction, the presence of heterogeneity in returns (or other sources of normative conflict) is a key moderator: control efficiency alone is not sufficient—one must also consider whether the game design induces normative conflict. Other design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, technology, player count) are held constant in this study, so the main actionable moderator is the symmetry/asymmetry of returns.

- source: Putterman, L., Tyran, J. R., & Kamei, K. (2011). Public goods and voting on formal sanction schemes. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when groups in a linear public goods game are allowed to vote on the parameters of a formal sanction scheme (who is penalized, maximum penalty, and exemption level), most groups quickly learn to select efficient penalty rules that induce full cooperation and maximize group earnings. In the baseline (no sanctions), contributions and earnings decline over time as expected. In the penalty treatment, after voting, contributions and earnings rise and are sustained at high levels, with the median group achieving full cooperation and maximum efficiency. The effect is attributed to the ability of groups to endogenously select strong, well-targeted penalties for free-riding. Individual differences (cooperative orientation, IQ, political conservatism, gender) predict voting behavior, but do not prevent most groups from converging to efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, direct evidence that enabling groups to endogenously design formal sanction schemes in a standard linear public goods game can transform a low-efficiency baseline into a high-efficiency outcome. The effect is robust to group heterogeneity and persists over repeated rounds. For prediction, the presence of a formal, group-voted punishment mechanism with high penalty rates and full exemption for full contributors is highly likely to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, provided the group can select the parameters. The findings are most applicable to settings with 5 players, 24 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and full information about group outcomes.

- source: Barclay, P. (2004). Trustworthiness and competitive altruism can also solve the tragedy of the commons. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment with Han Chinese and Mongolian Chinese students uses a standard public goods game with and without a costly punishment stage. Each group has 4 players, and each subject plays 10 rounds without punishment and 10 rounds with punishment. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR) is 0.4. Punishment costs 1 token to the punisher and reduces the punished's payoff by 3 tokens. The main findings are: (1) Punishment increases mean contribution in all treatments, but the efficiency of punishment (measured as the increase in contribution per unit of punishment) varies by group composition. (2) In all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups, punishment is efficient (marginal effect > 1), while in all-Mongolian groups, punishment is inefficient (marginal effect < 1). (3) Ethnic diversity (mixed groups) boosts punishment efficiency for both Han and Mongolian Chinese. (4) Mongolian Chinese punish more harshly within their own group due to cultural norms ('Anda' tradition), leading to lower efficiency. (5) Han Chinese show strong ingroup bias, contributing more in all-Han groups, especially with punishment. (6) Cultural fusion (Mongolian Chinese with Han cultural background) increases contribution in mixed groups. (7) Overall, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in all-Han and mixed groups, but not in all-Mongolian groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of introducing punishment on efficiency in public goods games with different group compositions. For games with 4 players, 10 rounds, MPCR 0.4, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 1:3, punishment increases efficiency in all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups (marginal effect > 1), but is inefficient in all-Mongolian groups (marginal effect < 1). If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, the expected efficiency with punishment will increase substantially in all-Han and mixed groups, but only modestly or not at all in all-Mongolian groups. The results are robust to a 'clear-cut' Han Chinese sample. The findings are most applicable to lab settings with similar design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/impact, no chat, continuous contribution, no rewards, anonymous punishment, and known round count). The paper is highly informative for the prediction task, especially regarding the moderating role of group composition and cultural background.

- source: Ertan, A., Page, T., & Putterman, L. (2009). Who to punish? Individual decisions and majority rule in mitigating the free rider problem. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when groups can vote on punishment rules, they overwhelmingly prohibit punishment of high contributors and converge toward allowing punishment of low contributors only. This rule structure leads to higher contributions and, crucially, higher efficiency (group earnings as a fraction of the maximum possible) compared to both no punishment and unrestricted punishment. The cost of punishment under unrestricted rules reduces efficiency, while targeted punishment of low contributors effectively deters free riding and increases group welfare. The findings are robust across two experimental designs (3-vote and 5-vote), and the efficiency gains are statistically significant. The study provides direct evidence that the structure and targeting of punishment, not just its presence, are critical for achieving efficiency gains in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency only if punishment is restricted to low contributors (i.e., free riders) and not allowed against high contributors. Unrestricted punishment can reduce efficiency due to costly 'perverse punishment.' The experimental design and results allow direct mapping to prediction tasks: for a 4-player, 30-round, no-chat, continuous-contribution VCM with MPCR 0.4, punishment cost 0.25 per 1 unit impact, and anonymous punishment, efficiency is highest when punishment is allowed only for low contributors. If the control (no punishment) efficiency is known, enabling targeted punishment (low-only) is likely to increase efficiency substantially; enabling unrestricted punishment may not. The findings are directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially regarding the targeting and scope of punishment.

- source: Page, T., Putterman, L., & Unel, B. (2005). Voluntary association in public goods experiments: Reciprocity, mimicry and efficiency. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment with baseline, punishment, regrouping, and combined treatments, both punishment and regrouping increase efficiency and contributions relative to baseline. Regrouping (voluntary association) yields higher efficiency than punishment, as punishment incurs direct costs to both punisher and punished. The combined treatment (regrouping + punishment) does not significantly outperform regrouping alone. The experiment also finds strong evidence of conditional cooperation and group sorting by contribution level. Efficiency rates are: Baseline 77%, Regrouping 88%, Punishment 81%, Combined 86%.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and MPCR of 0.4, adding a punishment mechanism (cost 0.25 per 1 unit punished, impact 1 per 0.25 spent) increases efficiency from 77% (baseline) to 81%. However, the efficiency gain is smaller than that from enabling voluntary regrouping (88%). The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment increases efficiency but the effect is limited by the costliness of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in similar lab PGGs.

- source: Traulsen, A., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2012). An economic experiment reveals that humans prefer pool punishment to maintain the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with five players per group, the introduction of pool punishment (especially with second-order punishment) increases cooperation and reduces defection, but leads to a significant loss in efficiency (average payoff per round drops by about a third). Peer punishment alone does not significantly affect efficiency, as punishment is rarely used when cooperation is high. When both peer and pool punishment are available, pool punishment dominates in the presence of second-order punishment, again with a substantial efficiency loss. The results support the theoretical prediction that pool punishment can stabilize cooperation but at the cost of group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard public goods game with five players, 25 rounds, no communication, binary (all-or-nothing) contributions, and known punishment costs and effectiveness, enabling pool punishment (especially with second-order punishment) reduces efficiency compared to the control (no or only peer punishment). The efficiency loss is substantial (about one third) when pool punishment is used with second-order punishment. Peer punishment alone does not significantly reduce efficiency. Thus, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment mechanism (peer vs pool), and whether second-order punishment is implemented, are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The specific parameterization (punishment cost, punishment impact, MPCR) is also informative for quantitative prediction.

- source: Egas, M., & Riedl, A. (2008). The economics of altruistic punishment and the maintenance of cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment only maintains cooperation when it is relatively effective (low cost to punisher, high impact on punished). However, even when cooperation is sustained, group and individual payoffs are lower than in the control without punishment, because the costs of punishment exceed the benefits from increased cooperation. The threshold of free-riding that goes unpunished increases as punishment becomes less effective. The main determinant of punishment's effect is the cost-to-impact ratio. The results suggest that altruistic punishment alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation efficiently, as it reduces group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard PGG with anonymous, one-shot (perfect stranger) interactions, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and can reduce it, especially when punishment is effective (low cost, high impact). The effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the cost-to-impact ratio: only when punishment is cheap and effective does it sustain cooperation, but even then, group payoffs are lower than in the no-punishment control. For prediction, this suggests that unless punishment is both cheap and highly effective, enabling punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency relative to control. The control efficiency (without punishment) is always higher than with punishment in this design. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions, especially punishment cost and effectiveness.

- source: Markussen, T., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2014). Self-Organization for Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Voting on Sanction Regimes. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with 5 players, groups could vote between no sanctions (NS), informal sanctions (IS: peer punishment), and formal sanctions (FS: centralized punishment with fixed and variable costs, deterrent or non-deterrent). Both IS and deterrent FS substantially increased group efficiency and earnings relative to NS. IS were highly cost-effective, yielding higher returns per point spent on sanctions than FS in most conditions. IS were especially effective when chosen by vote, with group earnings and efficiency about 30% higher than when IS were imposed exogenously. Non-deterrent FS also increased efficiency when chosen by vote, but less so than IS. High fixed costs for FS reduced both their efficiency and their popularity. Reducing the effectiveness of IS (increasing cost per unit punishment) decreased their efficiency somewhat, but IS remained popular and relatively effective. Allowing IS and FS to coexist did not substantially change these patterns. Overall, IS can be as or more effective than FS in promoting efficiency, especially when adopted democratically.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with 5 players, enabling informal peer punishment (IS) can increase group efficiency to levels comparable to or exceeding those achieved by formal sanctions, especially when IS are chosen by vote. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost-effectiveness of the sanction regime, the fixed and variable costs of punishment, and whether the regime is adopted endogenously. High fixed costs for FS reduce their efficiency advantage. IS are most effective when punishment is relatively cheap and well-targeted. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling IS is likely to yield a substantial increase in efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, and that the effect is robust to moderate changes in punishment technology and institutional coexistence. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is typically well below full cooperation, while IS and deterrent FS can approach or reach high efficiency, with IS often being more cost-effective.

- source: Bochet, O., & Putterman, L. (2009). Not just babble: Opening the black box of communication in a voluntary contribution experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the addition of a costly punishment stage increases contributions but not efficiency, as punishment costs offset the gains. Allowing non-binding numerical announcements or promises alone does not improve efficiency or contributions. However, when the ability to make non-binding promises is combined with punishment, both contributions and group earnings (efficiency) increase significantly. This is because the threat of punishment for broken promises makes communication credible, reducing the frequency of lying and the need for actual costly punishment. Thus, the combination of communication (promises) and punishment is more effective for efficiency than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players, 10 rounds, and a marginal per capita return of 0.4, the addition of costly punishment increases contributions but not efficiency, due to the cost of punishment. However, if the game also allows for non-binding promise statements (in addition to punishment and numerical communication), efficiency increases significantly, as credible communication reduces the need for costly punishment. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the communication environment: punishment alone does not improve efficiency, but punishment plus credible communication (promises) does. The paper also provides detailed evidence on the cost structure of punishment and the behavioral mechanisms involved.

- source: Gintis, H., Bowles, S., Boyd, R., & Fehr, E. (2003). Explaining altruistic behavior in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Experimental public goods games consistently show that when participants can punish defectors at a personal cost, cooperation rates remain high or even increase over time, and group payoffs (efficiency) are substantially higher than when punishment is not allowed. In the Fehr & Gächter (2000) experiments, for example, allowing punishment led to a 40% increase in net payoffs to all subjects, even after accounting for the costs of punishment. The effect is observed even under strict anonymity and in one-shot or non-repeated interactions, indicating that the mechanism is not driven by expectations of future reciprocity. The paper also reviews cross-cultural evidence and evolutionary models, but the core experimental result is that punishment opportunities robustly improve group efficiency in public goods game environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across standard lab PGG designs with 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR. The magnitude of the efficiency gain is substantial (e.g., 40% increase in net payoffs in Fehr & Gächter 2000). The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency given the control game design and efficiency, especially in standard PGG settings. The paper also discusses the importance of group structure (e.g., partner vs. stranger matching), but the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is consistent across these variations.

- source: Kosfeld, M., Okada, A., & Riedl, A. (2009). Institution Formation in Public Goods Games. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when groups can endogenously form a centralized sanctioning institution (which punishes non-contributors among its members), they do so in a large majority of cases, and most often as a 'grand organization' including all players. This leads to much higher and more stable contributions and efficiency compared to control groups without the institution option. In the lower MPCR treatment (a=0.4), efficiency rises from 25% (control) to 51% (institution formation); in the higher MPCR treatment (a=0.65), efficiency rises from 62% (control) to 70% (institution formation), though the latter difference is not statistically significant. The institution is only implemented if all players join, reflecting strong fairness concerns. When institution formation fails, efficiency is not lower than in the control. The results show that endogenous punishment institutions can solve the public goods dilemma and raise efficiency, but their success depends on fairness-driven willingness to include all group members.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling centralized punishment institutions in linear public goods games. When the control (no punishment) efficiency is low, enabling institution formation can more than double efficiency, especially at lower MPCR. The effect is robust to group size (n=4), number of rounds (20), and the specific centralized punishment structure used here. The main moderator is the MPCR: the efficiency gain is larger when MPCR is lower. The institution is only effective if all players join, so settings that facilitate or require universal participation are more likely to see large efficiency gains. The cost of the institution (here, 2 points per round shared among members) is low relative to the efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency in similar lab PGGs with centralized punishment options.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2014). Democratic decisions establish stable authorities that overcome the paradox of second-order punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that second-order punishment institutions (which punish both defectors and those who do not support the punishment institution) result in higher average payoffs and cooperation rates than institutions without second-order punishment. When given a choice, groups using majority voting overwhelmingly select the more efficient second-order punishment institution, while groups using individual migration (foot voting) initially avoid it, resulting in lower payoffs. Over time, some foot-voting groups shift toward the more efficient institution, but not as consistently as with majority voting. The findings highlight the importance of democratic decision-making in establishing efficient punishment institutions that sustain cooperation and maximize group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling second-order punishment in a public goods game increases efficiency (average payoff) compared to a control without such punishment, under otherwise identical game parameters. The effect is robust across both theoretical predictions and experimental results. The findings also show that the method of institutional choice (majority voting vs. individual migration) moderates the realized efficiency, with majority voting more reliably leading to the efficient outcome. For prediction tasks, the presence of second-order punishment, the method of institutional choice, and the group’s prior experience with institutional regimes are important moderators. The paper’s detailed reporting of payoffs under both control and treatment conditions makes it directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Fehr, E., & Gächter, S. (2002). Altruistic punishment in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with random group assignment and no reputation or repeated interaction, the opportunity for costly punishment (1 MU cost to punisher, 3 MU cost to target per point) leads to a large and sustained increase in average contributions compared to a no-punishment baseline. Most punishment is directed at below-average contributors by above-average contributors. The threat and execution of punishment both increase cooperation. Individual income is maximized when contributions are close to the group average, and both positive and negative deviations reduce income. The emotional mechanism underlying punishment is negative affect toward free riders. The paper does not report group efficiency as a percentage of the social optimum, but the data and payoff structure imply that efficiency is much higher with punishment enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard linear public goods game with four players, six rounds, no communication, continuous contribution choice, and no reputation or repeated interaction. The control (no-punishment) condition shows declining contributions and low efficiency, while the punishment condition shows increasing contributions and much higher efficiency. The punishment mechanism is costly (1:3 cost:impact), and punishment is anonymous. The findings support the prediction that, in similar designs, enabling punishment will substantially increase group efficiency relative to the control. The paper does not manipulate other design dimensions, so its predictive value is strongest for games matching its structure.

- source: Herrmann, B., Thöni, C., & Gächter, S. (2008). Antisocial punishment across societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a punishment option in a repeated public goods game increases cooperation and group earnings in some societies, but not in others. In societies with high levels of antisocial punishment, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation and group earnings is diminished or absent. The variation in outcomes is strongly associated with societal-level factors such as norms of civic cooperation and the strength of the rule of law. The study demonstrates that punishment is not universally beneficial for efficiency or cooperation; its effect depends on the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which varies widely across societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly context-dependent. When predicting the efficiency of a game with punishment enabled, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions but also the likelihood of antisocial punishment, which may be influenced by societal or group-level norms. In environments similar to those with low antisocial punishment (e.g., Western Europe, US), punishment is likely to increase efficiency. In environments with high antisocial punishment, punishment may have little or even negative effect on efficiency. The game design dimensions in this study (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, anonymity) are held constant, so the main moderator is the social context, not the game mechanics. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless antisocial punishment is low.

- source: Dal Bó, E., & Dal Bó, P. (2014). Do the right thing: The effects of moral suasion on cooperation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a series of lab experiments using a two-player linear public goods game, the authors show that (1) moral suasion (exposure to a moral message) increases contributions and group payoffs, but the effect is short-lived when punishment is not available; (2) when a punishment stage is added (where players can pay to reduce their partner's payoff), the effect of moral suasion becomes persistent, with higher and sustained contributions and higher group payoffs; (3) punishment alone increases contributions compared to no-punishment, but does not prevent a decline over time; (4) the combination of punishment and moral suasion produces the highest and most persistent cooperation and payoffs; (5) the mechanisms include both changes in expectations about others and changes in preferences. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, but does report group payoffs and their changes, which are closely related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player linear public goods game with random rematching, adding a punishment stage (with a cost to the punisher and a larger cost to the punished) increases group payoffs and sustains higher cooperation, especially when combined with moral suasion. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive, and the effect is amplified when moral suasion is present. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, etc.) are well specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with and without punishment. The paper is less informative about settings with more players, field contexts, or different punishment technologies, but is highly informative for standard lab PGGs.

- source: Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. (2016). Partner choice versus punishment in human Prisoner's Dilemmas. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four studies, the paper finds that (1) punishers are rated as more trustworthy and group focused than nonpunishers; (2) after repeated PGG rounds, punishers are trusted more and receive more money in trust games, but this effect is absent after a single round; (3) justified punishment (punishing free riders) leads to increased trust and monetary benefits, while unjustified punishment leads to reduced trust and earnings; (4) the reputational and monetary benefits of justified punishment can offset or even exceed the cost of punishing, especially in repeated interactions. The paper does not directly report group efficiency ratios, but does show that punishment can increase individual and group monetary outcomes via increased trust and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated PGGs with punishment, justified punishment can increase the monetary benefits to punishers via enhanced trust and subsequent transfers, partially or fully offsetting the cost of punishment. The effect is contingent on repeated interaction (number of rounds) and the justification for punishment.
