For prediction tasks about the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-costly, decentralized exclusion can promote cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or about the effect of costly punishment. The game design dimensions are partially informative (e.g., player count, network updating rules), but the absence of explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes and standard punishment limits direct applicability.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: De Cremer, D., & van Dijk, E. (2009). Paying for sanctions in social dilemmas: The effects of endowment asymmetry and accountability. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in a step-level public good dilemma, high-endowment members contribute proportionally more to the public good than low-endowment members, regardless of accountability. However, when it comes to paying for a sanctioning system (which is framed as a second-order, continuous public good dilemma without a threshold), high-endowment members do not contribute more than low-endowment members unless they are made accountable and expect to be morally evaluated by the group. Accountability and evaluation expectancy increase contributions to the sanctioning system among high-endowment members, but these effects are not observed for contributions to the public good itself. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of these manipulations on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the psychological and social moderators of willingness to pay for punishment systems in public good dilemmas, especially under endowment asymmetry. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that accountability and evaluation expectancy can increase willingness to pay for sanctions among high-endowment members, but the translation of this effect to efficiency or group payoff is not established. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about the behavioral mechanisms that might affect the implementation of punishment systems.

- source: Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2011). Vigilant Self-Regulation, Cues of Being Watched and Cooperativeness. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that a cue of being watched (reputational cue) increases cooperative behavior (donation to a public good) among individuals with a strong prevention focus, but decreases it among those with a weak prevention focus. The effect is specific to prevention focus; promotion focus does not predict donation behavior. The manipulation is not punishment or sanctioning, but rather a reputational cue. The outcome is individual donation, not group efficiency or payoff. No repeated interaction, no explicit public goods game structure, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The design is a one-shot donation game with a reputational cue, not a repeated PGG with punishment. The findings may be relevant for understanding how reputational cues (such as being watched) interact with individual differences to affect cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The absence of punishment, reward, or group payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Waite, A. J., & Shou, W. Y. (2012). Adaptation to a new environment allows cooperators to purge cheaters stochastically. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a well-mixed, engineered yeast system where cooperators pay a cost to produce a common good and cheaters do not, adaptation to a novel, nutrient-limited environment can allow cooperators to stochastically dominate and purge cheaters in some replicate cultures. This outcome is driven by the chance acquisition of highly beneficial mutations in cooperators, which can offset the initial fitness advantage of cheaters. The frequency of cooperator-dominated cultures is determined by the initial abundance of cooperators. The paper does not study or manipulate punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in the sense relevant to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings about evolutionary adaptation and stochastic outcomes in microbial cooperation are only weakly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in human or animal public goods games. The main value is in illustrating that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., adaptation to novel environments) can allow cooperation to persist, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: Stoop, J., Noussair, C. N., & Van Soest, D. (2012). From the Lab to the Field: Cooperation among Fishermen. *Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that, in a real-world field setting where cooperation would require fishermen to forgo catching fish (an activity they enjoy), no cooperation emerges, and group efficiency does not improve relative to the control. This is in contrast to standard lab VCM experiments, where positive cooperation and higher efficiency are typically observed. The lack of cooperation is robust to subject pool, context, and setting, and is attributed to the nature of the activity required for cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, so the paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a high-quality control case for field VCM-like games without punishment: efficiency remains at the non-cooperative baseline, with no spontaneous cooperation. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is implemented or analyzed. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful only for establishing the baseline (control) efficiency in a field VCM-like environment with no punishment. It does not provide evidence on how punishment would change efficiency in this context.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J. R., & Milinski, M. (2003). Volunteering leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics in a public goods game. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to opt out of the public goods game (become 'loners') leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This dynamic sustains a stable, nonzero level of cooperation and prevents the collapse to universal defection typically seen in standard public goods games. Average payoffs for cooperators and defectors converge to the loner payoff over time, as predicted by theory. The presence of the loner option increases the average frequency of cooperation compared to standard PGGs, but does not produce overwhelming cooperation. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that introducing an opt-out (volunteering/loner) option can sustain cooperation and stabilize payoffs in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (volunteering) that can also affect efficiency.

- source: Mak, V., Zwick, R., Rao, A. R., & Pattaratanakun, J. A. (2015). Pay what you want as threshold public good provision. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that in a repeated threshold public good environment, pre-payment chat (communication) among players enables highly efficient coordination, resulting in sustained provision of the public good (PWYW) for most rounds. Without chat, groups fail to coordinate and the public good is rarely sustained. The effect of chat is robust to variations in feedback (full vs. partial information about others' payments) and market information (knowledge of player types). The main outcome is group efficiency, operationalized as the proportion of rounds in which the threshold is met and the public good continues. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism; instead, efficiency is achieved through communication and social contracting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public good games without punishment, enabling pre-play communication (chat) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when other forms of information are limited. For prediction tasks, the presence of chat should be treated as a major moderator of efficiency in repeated threshold public good environments. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly informative about the effect of punishment per se, but they do establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication and no punishment. Control efficiency (no chat) is low; treatment efficiency (with chat) is high. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, etc.) are held constant, so the main actionable dimension is chat.

- source: Kameda, T., Tsukasaki, T., Hastie, R., & Berg, N. (2011). Democracy Under Uncertainty: The Wisdom of Crowds and the Free-Rider Problem in Group Decision Making. *Psychological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in group decision-making tasks with diminishing marginal returns (a nonlinear public goods environment), cooperation and free-riding coexist in a stable mixed equilibrium. In both simulation and laboratory experiment (with 6-person groups, 24 rounds, no punishment or reward), about half the group members consistently cooperate (paying a cost to acquire information and vote), while the rest free ride. The majority/plurality rule for aggregating decisions yields higher individual net payoffs (efficiency) than a best-member (dictator) rule, provided cooperation costs are not too high. The equilibrium level of cooperation is locally Pareto efficient, with three contributors and three free riders per group. The study demonstrates that efficient group outcomes can be achieved without punishment, provided the group production function is nonlinear with diminishing returns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with diminishing marginal returns and no punishment, stable and efficient cooperation can emerge. The findings suggest that the pessimistic predictions of linear public goods games (where efficiency collapses without punishment) do not generalize to more realistic nonlinear settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper indicates that the efficiency of the control game (without punishment) can be high if the production function is nonlinear and the group decision rule is majoritarian, even in the absence of punishment. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Therefore, it should be used as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in nonlinear group production environments.

- source: Capraro, V., Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Heuristics guide the implementation of social preferences in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma experiments. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that in a one-shot, continuous-strategy PD, cooperation is trimodally distributed (none, half, all). Increasing the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c) increases the average amount of cooperation by shifting people from no cooperation to full cooperation, but does not affect the proportion of 50% cooperators. The results suggest that both efficiency concerns and heuristic reasoning (such as fairness heuristics) guide cooperation. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The study is relevant for understanding how b/c affects cooperation in two-player, one-shot, continuous public goods settings, but does not inform about punishment or efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide informative evidence about how the marginal per-capita return (b/c) affects cooperation rates in a two-player, one-shot, continuous-contribution setting without punishment. It shows that higher b/c increases cooperation, but a substantial fraction of participants use heuristics (e.g., giving half) regardless of b/c. Since there is no punishment or efficiency outcome reported, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation as a function of b/c in the absence of punishment.

- source: Tazelaar, M. J. A., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Ouwerkerk, J. W. (2004). How to cope with Noise, in social dilemmas: The benefits of communication. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that negative noise (randomly reducing the partner's intended contribution) significantly reduces cooperation and benign impressions in a repeated two-player give-some game. Allowing the partner to communicate about noise (explaining/apologizing for the noise) restores cooperation and benign impressions to levels indistinguishable from the no-noise baseline. These effects are especially pronounced for participants with low dispositional trust. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and psychological impressions, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide strong evidence that communication can mitigate the negative effects of noise on cooperation in repeated continuous-contribution dilemmas. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding the role of communication and noise, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment.

- source: Barclay, P., & Barker, J. L. (2020). Greener Than Thou: People who protect the environment are more cooperative, compete to be environmental, and benefit from reputation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) people who engage in more pro-environmental behaviors are more likely to cooperate in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma; (2) people donate more to an environmental charity when their donations are observed, and even more when competing to be chosen as a partner; (3) observers preferentially choose and cooperate more with those who donated more to the environment; (4) the correlation between environmentalism and cooperation is partially mediated by concern for others. The study does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of observability and partner choice (reputation/competition) on cooperative and pro-environmental behavior in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For prediction tasks involving the effects of observability or partner choice on cooperation, the findings suggest that such mechanisms can increase cooperative behaviors, but no efficiency or payoff data are available.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Contagion of Cooperation in Static and Fluid Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in fixed and viscous networks, both cooperation and defection are contagious: individuals are more likely to switch to cooperation (or defection) if their neighbors did so in the previous round. In fluid networks, where players can more frequently change their partners, cooperation is not initially contagious—players are likely to cooperate regardless of their neighbors' behavior, likely to attract new cooperative partners. As the network stabilizes and becomes less fluid, contagion of cooperation emerges. Defection remains contagious in all network types. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show how network structure and partner choice affect the contagion of cooperation and defection in binary cooperation games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as context for how network fluidity and partner choice may interact with behavioral contagion. It does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Buchan, N. R., Grimalda, G., Wilson, R., Brewer, M., Fatas, E., & Foddy, M. (2009). Globalization and human cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both country-level and individual-level globalization are strongly positively associated with contributions to the global public good (World account) in a multilevel public goods experiment. There is no evidence that globalization is associated with increased local cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The main finding is that globalization predicts broader, more cosmopolitan cooperation, as measured by willingness to contribute to a global public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are about the relationship between globalization and willingness to contribute to global versus local public goods, not about the effect of game design dimensions or punishment on efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Conflict, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both state trust (expectations of others' cooperation) and dispositional trust are positively correlated with cooperation in social dilemmas. The strength of this relationship is moderated by the degree of conflict in the game: trust is a stronger predictor of cooperation when the conflict of interest is higher (lower index of cooperation). The type of social dilemma (public goods, prisoner's dilemma, resource dilemma) does not significantly moderate the trust-cooperation relationship. Other moderators (iterations, group size, country, intergroup vs. individual) are also explored. The paper does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about how the structure of the game (degree of conflict, as measured by the index of cooperation) moderates the relationship between trust and cooperation. Since efficiency is not measured, and punishment is not studied, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, but may be useful for understanding how baseline cooperation varies with game structure in the absence of punishment.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2009). Cooperation among Strangers under the Shadow of the Future. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In small, randomly matched groups playing indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, enabling costly personal punishment (where a player can pay 5 points to reduce the opponent's payoff by 10) in an otherwise anonymous, privately monitored environment increases both cooperation rates and realized efficiency. Specifically, average realized efficiency rises from 0.595 (private monitoring, no punishment) to 0.652 (private monitoring with punishment). The effect is comparable to that of making individual histories public (efficiency 0.815), though the efficiency gain from punishment is partially offset by the cost of punishment itself. The paper also finds that simply making aggregate histories public (without identities) does not improve efficiency. Subjects tend to use selective punishment strategies, and personal punishment is often used as a substitute for informal punishment (defecting in future rounds). Larger group sizes (14 vs. 4) lead to much lower cooperation and efficiency, suggesting strong group size effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for the effect of enabling costly personal punishment in indefinitely repeated, small-group, random-matching prisoner's dilemma environments. The design is adjacent to public goods games but not identical (pairwise PD rather than n-player PGG), so results should be transferred with some caution. The key finding is that, holding all else constant, enabling punishment increases realized efficiency substantially (from 0.595 to 0.652), though not as much as making individual histories public (0.815). The cost of punishment reduces the efficiency gain relative to the increase in cooperation. The paper also shows that group size is a strong moderator: larger groups have much lower efficiency. For prediction, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in small, anonymous, privately monitored repeated games, with the caveat that the effect is partly offset by the costliness of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings with 4 players, random matching, no chat, continuous contribution/defection, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 5/10.

- source: Wu, J. J., Zhang, B. Y., Zhou, Z. X., He, Q. Q., Zheng, X. D., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2009). Costly punishment does not always increase cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with Chinese participants, the introduction of costly punishment either had no significant effect or reduced the frequency of cooperation, and led to a significant decrease in average group payoff (efficiency) in one treatment. In the other treatment, the decrease in payoff was not statistically significant. The results are contrasted with previous US-based studies, where costly punishment increased cooperation but did not improve group payoff. The authors attribute the difference to cultural factors, particularly attitudes toward reputation and authority. The study provides direct evidence that costly punishment does not always increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even reduce them depending on context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game), enabling costly punishment can reduce group efficiency (average payoff), especially when cooperation does not increase. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on cultural context and game structure. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and in some settings (e.g., repeated dyadic games, certain cultures), it may even lower it. The paper also highlights that increased cooperation does not always translate to higher efficiency if punishment costs offset cooperative gains.

- source: Dreber, A., Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Nowak, M. A. (2008). Winners don't punish. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishment option in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma increases the frequency of cooperation but does not increase the average payoff of the group. In both parameterizations (benefit/cost ratios of 2 and 3), there is no significant difference in average payoff between the control (no punishment) and treatment (punishment allowed) conditions. Individuals who use punishment more frequently have lower total payoffs, and the most successful players do not use punishment at all. The results suggest that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games, punishment is maladaptive from an efficiency perspective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games with direct reciprocity, enabling costly punishment does not improve group efficiency compared to a control without punishment. The cost of punishment cancels out the gains from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar repeated dyadic games, the efficiency with punishment enabled will be similar to or lower than the control efficiency, even if cooperation rates rise. The findings are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma designs, but the mechanism (costs of punishment offsetting cooperation gains) is likely relevant for other repeated social dilemma games with similar structure.

- source: Chen, Y., & Li, S. X. (2009). Group Identity and Social Preferences. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This laboratory experiment systematically measures the effects of induced group identity on social preferences and economic outcomes in a set of 24 two-person sequential allocation games (including dictator and response games with punishment/reward options). The key findings are: (1) participants are more likely to make social-welfare-maximizing (SWM) choices and earn higher payoffs when matched with ingroup members, and less likely to do so with outgroup members, compared to a control with no group identity; (2) participants are more likely to reward ingroup members for good behavior and less likely to punish them for misbehavior, while the opposite holds for outgroup members; (3) the gap in efficiency/payoff between ingroup and outgroup matches is driven more by losses in outgroup matches than gains in ingroup matches. The study also finds that the presence of group identity (even when randomly assigned) is sufficient to generate these effects, and that design features like chat can increase group attachment but have limited behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: punishment is less likely to be used against ingroup members (reducing efficiency losses from antisocial punishment), but more likely against outgroup members (increasing efficiency losses). The presence of group identity can thus moderate the efficiency impact of punishment, with ingroup contexts supporting higher efficiency and outgroup contexts supporting lower efficiency, relative to a no-identity baseline. The findings suggest that group composition and identity salience are important moderators for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment mechanisms, even in games that are not classic PGGs but share key features (sequential allocation, punishment/reward options, payoff externalities).

- source: Abbink, K., Brandts, J., Herrmann, B., & Orzen, H. (2010). Intergroup Conflict and Intra-Group Punishment in an Experimental Contest Game. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a group contest game, the introduction of within-group punishment opportunities causes a large increase in unproductive conflict expenditures and a severe reduction in efficiency. In the baseline (no-punishment) group contest, expenditures are already well above equilibrium, but with punishment, they rise even further: in the last five rounds, contest expenditures are 119% higher than without punishment, and total material losses (including punishment costs) are 869% of the Nash equilibrium level, with rent dissipation exceeding 100% of the prize. Punishment is used primarily to target low contributors, and its presence reduces heterogeneity in team member contributions, but the net effect is to discipline members into even more wasteful over-investment. The results directly contradict the efficiency-enhancing effects of punishment observed in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in contest games with public-good-like intra-group sharing, enabling within-group punishment leads to a large decrease in efficiency, with group payoffs and welfare falling far below both the equilibrium and the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust across rounds and is not offset by any increase in group earnings. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment in a group contest environment should be expected to reduce efficiency, even if punishment increases contributions or reduces free-riding. The game design dimensions most relevant here are group size, number of rounds, the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the contest structure (lottery contest for a rival prize with intra-group sharing). Control efficiency (without punishment) is already low, but enabling punishment makes it much worse.

- source: Ule, A., Schram, A., Riedl, A., & Cason, T. N. (2009). Indirect Punishment and Generosity Toward Strangers. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments of an indirect helping game: one where indirect punishment is only symbolic (no material effect on the recipient), and one where punishment is harmful (recipient loses 250 francs). In both, helping is costly and beneficial, and donors can observe recipients' recent behavior. The main finding is that, although the frequency of helping does not differ significantly between treatments, the availability of harmful punishment reduces the payoffs of defectors and increases the payoffs of rewarders and cautious defectors. In the control, defectors earn most; with harmful punishment, rewarders and cautious defectors earn more than defectors. Harmful punishment is rare but targeted at defectors, and this is sufficient to change the payoff structure in favor of cooperation-supporting strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly, effective punishment in an indirect reciprocity environment (adjacent to PGG) increases the average payoff of cooperative strategies relative to defectors, even if overall cooperation rates do not rise. For prediction, this suggests that, in games where punishment is enabled and is materially costly to the recipient, group efficiency or average payoff can increase relative to a control with only symbolic or no punishment, primarily by reducing defectors' payoffs. The effect is not driven by increased cooperation rates but by the deterrent effect of punishment on defectors' earnings. The design is not a standard PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, punishment, and payoff outcomes.

- source: Li, X. L., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., Li, H. J., Shi, L., Podobnik, B., Stanley, H. E., Havlin, S., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Punishment diminishes the benefits of network reciprocity in social dilemma experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared three treatments: (1) a well-mixed repeated PD (control), (2) a static network PD with two neighbors (network reciprocity), and (3) a static network PD with punishment available. Network reciprocity increased both cooperation and payoff relative to the well-mixed control. However, adding punishment did not further increase cooperation and actually reduced the median payoff per round back to the control level. Punishment also reduced assortment (clustering of cooperators) and, in larger neighborhoods, erased the positive correlation between cooperation and payoff. Thus, punishment in this setting is ineffective or even counterproductive for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games on static networks (with two or four neighbors), enabling costly punishment (with a 1:4 cost-to-impact ratio) does not improve and may even reduce group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The negative effect is especially clear in payoff-based outcomes: median payoff per round is lower with punishment than without, and the benefit of network reciprocity is erased. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar networked PD or adjacent PGG-like environments, adding punishment may not increase efficiency and could reduce it, especially as neighborhood size increases. The findings are most directly applicable to static network PDs with small groups, no communication, and fixed payoff matrices.

- source: Lergetporer, P., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Sutter, M. (2014). Third-party punishment increases cooperation in children through (misaligned) expectations and conditional cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD with children increases cooperation rates from 25% (control) to 58% (punishment treatment). This leads to an increase in average payoffs from 2.49 tokens (control) to 2.98 tokens (punishment), a 20% improvement in efficiency. The effect is not due to frequent actual punishment (which is rare), but rather to children's (over-)expectation of punishment and to conditional cooperation based on higher expected partner cooperation. The effect is robust across age cohorts (7-11 years).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a two-player, one-shot PD with children substantially increases efficiency, even when actual punishment is rare. The effect is mediated by beliefs and conditional cooperation. For prediction, the presence of a third-party punishment mechanism (with a strong, binary punishment technology) in a simple, anonymous, one-shot, two-player game with no communication and known payoffs can be expected to increase efficiency by a substantial margin (here, ~20%). The effect is robust to age (within 7-11), and is not dependent on actual punishment frequency. The design is adjacent to a PGG, but the payoff structure and mechanism are closely related.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Wang, R. W., Shi, L., Iwasa, Y., Moreno, Y., & Kurths, J. (2017). Onymity promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with three available actions (cooperate, defect, punish), the use of costly punishment is negatively correlated with individual payoffs in both anonymous and onymous conditions. Onymity (making opponents identifiable) increases cooperation and median payoffs, but punishment does not promote efficiency or cooperation. Instead, those who punish more tend to earn less ('winners don't punish' effect). Control trials without punishment show similar qualitative effects of onymity, suggesting that punishment is not necessary for increased cooperation or efficiency. The paper provides direct evidence that punishment, as implemented here, does not improve group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. In fact, punishment is associated with lower payoffs for those who use it. The findings suggest that, all else equal, adding a costly punishment option to a similar game design is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may even reduce it. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, discrete-choice social dilemmas with costly punishment, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to multi-player public goods games. However, the negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust in this setting.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Reuben, E. (2009). The Importance of Emotions for the Effectiveness of Social Punishment*. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing punishment in a sequential social dilemma (trust-like game) increases cooperation and the amount returned by second movers, compared to a baseline without punishment. However, punishment also triggers retaliation, leading to multiple rounds of costly punishment and counter-punishment. This escalation increases the cost of enforcing cooperation and can destroy resources, sometimes offsetting the gains from increased cooperation. The emotional mechanisms underlying these behaviors are explored: anger motivates punishment and retaliation, while guilt and shame can inhibit retaliation and promote pro-social adjustment. The presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee higher group earnings or efficiency, as the costs of punishment and retaliation can outweigh the benefits of increased cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in games where punishment is possible and both parties can retaliate, the effect of punishment on efficiency is ambiguous: while punishment can increase cooperation, the associated costs (especially from retaliation) can reduce or even negate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence of punishment does not always lead to higher efficiency, especially in settings where retaliation is possible and costly. The design dimensions most relevant here are the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the possibility of retaliation, and the structure of the game (sequential, two-player, not a standard PGG). Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher in the absence of costly conflict, but may be lower due to less cooperation. When punishment is enabled, efficiency may increase or decrease depending on the balance between increased cooperation and resource destruction from punishment cycles.

- source: De Dreu, C. K. W., Gross, J., Méder, Z., Giffin, M., Prochazkova, E., Krikeb, J., & Columbus, S. (2016). In-group defense, out-group aggression, and coordination failures in intergroup conflict. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases contributions to out-group aggression but does not improve the aggressor group's success rate or overall group payoffs; it leads to more wasted resources and lower individual wealth. In contrast, sequential decision-making (a coordination mechanism) increases coordination within aggressor groups, nearly doubles their success rate, and reduces wealth loss for aggressors (but increases it for defenders). The results suggest that punishment can motivate higher contributions but does not solve the coordination problem or improve efficiency, while coordination mechanisms can enhance group success and payoffs in competitive intergroup settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest games with costly contributions and the possibility of peer punishment, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve group efficiency or payoffs—indeed, it can reduce them due to the direct costs of punishment. Coordination mechanisms (like sequential decision-making) are more effective at improving group success and payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment (with the cost/impact structure used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and may even decrease it. The effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the coordination context and the cost/impact structure of punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2003). Detrimental effects of sanctions on human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a one-shot sequential trust game, allowing the investor to impose a fine on the trustee for insufficient back-transfer leads to lower back-transfers from trustees and lower payoffs for investors, compared to both a baseline with no sanctions and to cases where the investor refrains from using the fine. The negative effect of sanctions is especially pronounced when the investor's desired back-transfer is high (i.e., when the sanction is used to enforce an unfair distribution). The paper interprets this as evidence that sanctions perceived as self-interested or unfair undermine altruistic cooperation, and thus can reduce overall efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a sequential trust game (not a standard PGG), enabling punishment (sanctions) can reduce efficiency-related outcomes (investor payoff, group surplus) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is perceived as self-interested or unfair. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: in trust games with one-shot interactions and potential for perceived unfairness, punishment may reduce efficiency. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGGs, but highlight the importance of the perceived legitimacy and purpose of punishment as a moderator.

- source: Fehr, E., Hoff, K., & Kshetramade, M. (2008). Spite and development. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper documents widespread spiteful punishment in a one-shot trust game with third-party punishment in rural India: 61–73% of third parties punished cooperators, not just defectors. This antisocial punishment reduces the expected returns to cooperation and undermines the efficiency benefits of punishment. In a repeated stag hunt game, high-caste pairs (who are more spiteful) were much less likely to coordinate on the efficient, high-payoff equilibrium than low-caste pairs. Dictator games show that high-caste subjects are more likely to engage in payoff-reducing, spiteful choices. The findings suggest that in environments where spiteful preferences are prevalent, punishment opportunities may fail to increase, or may even decrease, group efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: when antisocial or spiteful punishment is common, punishment can reduce or fail to improve efficiency, even in games structurally similar to public goods or trust games. The presence of spiteful preferences, as measured by the frequency of punishment of cooperators and by dictator game choices, is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in settings with high antisocial punishment or spite, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and may even decrease it, especially if punishment is not tightly targeted to defectors. Game design dimensions that allow for antisocial punishment (e.g., anonymity, lack of accountability, or cultural context) should be considered as risk factors for negative efficiency effects.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., Kurzban, R., & McCullough, M. E. (2013). Do humans really punish altruistically? A closer look. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a lab setting where third-party punishment is possible but demand characteristics and audience effects are minimized, witnesses of unfairness toward others rarely engage in costly punishment. When they do, it is not motivated by moralistic anger but by envy. Victims of unfairness (those personally harmed) do punish at nontrivial rates. In hypothetical scenarios, people forecast much more punishment and anger than is observed in real behavior. The results challenge the robustness of altruistic third-party punishment in humans and suggest that previous findings may be due to experimental artefacts. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dictator/third-party punishment games where the punisher is not personally harmed, actual costly punishment is rare and mild, and not motivated by norm-enforcing anger. This suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff, since punishment is rarely used and not directed at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, and the findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is only available to third parties who are not personally affected. The results caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in such designs.

- source: Martin, J. W., & Cushman, F. (2015). To Punish or to Leave: Distinct Cognitive Processes Underlie Partner Control and Partner Choice Behaviors. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dyadic allocation game with stochastic outcomes, punishment (partner control) is influenced by both the allocator's intent and the actual outcome, while partner choice (switching partners) is influenced almost exclusively by intent. The design allows for the separation of intent and outcome by having allocators choose between dice with different probabilities of fair/selfish outcomes. Responders in the punishment condition could add or subtract money (costlessly, with low probability of effect), while in the partner choice condition they could choose to stay or switch partners. The main dependent variables are the behavioral responses and their sensitivity to intent/outcome. No group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological determinants of punishment and partner choice behaviors, showing that punishment is sensitive to both intent and outcome, while partner choice is intent-driven. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may inform understanding of when punishment is likely to be used and what cues drive it.

- source: Andreoni, J., Harbaugh, W., & Vesterlund, L. (2003). The carrot or the stick: Rewards, punishments, and cooperation. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a two-person proposer-responder game, the ability to punish (reduce the proposer's payoff at a cost) increases the amount proposers share with responders, compared to a baseline with no punishment or reward. The ability to reward (increase the proposer's payoff at a cost) alone has little effect on the modal offer, though it increases variance and leads a minority to be very generous. When both punishment and reward are available, proposers make the most generous offers, and the distribution of offers first-order stochastically dominates the other treatments. Punishment and reward act as complements: punishment moves people away from selfishness, and reward encourages further cooperation. The study does not use a standard public goods game, but the findings are relevant for understanding how punishment and reward options affect cooperative behavior and, by implication, group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person proposer-responder environment, enabling punishment increases cooperative behavior (higher offers), and that combining punishment and reward is even more effective. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings suggest that, for similar environments, adding punishment (and especially both punishment and reward) will increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither. The effect of reward alone is weaker. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, one-shot or randomly rematched games with costly punishment and reward options. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that punishment (especially when combined with reward) will increase efficiency or group payoff, even if the effect of reward alone is limited.

- source: Gneezy, A., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2012). Conflict, sticks and carrots: war increases prosocial punishments and rewards. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of violent intergroup conflict, individuals are more willing to incur costs to punish non-cooperative group members (as measured by higher rejection rates of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game) and to reward cooperative group members (as measured by higher returns in the Trust Game when the first mover is generous). These effects are observed by comparing behavior before, during, and after the 2006 Israel-Hezbollah war among Israeli senior citizens. The findings support the hypothesis that intergroup conflict elevates sanctioning behaviors that promote within-group cooperation. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on the behavioral propensity to punish and reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that real-world intergroup conflict increases individuals' willingness to punish non-cooperators and reward cooperators in economic games structurally related to public goods dilemmas. However, because the games used (Ultimatum and Trust Games) are not public goods games and the outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that contextual factors (such as intergroup conflict) can increase sanctioning behaviors, which, in public goods games, are often associated with higher cooperation and sometimes higher efficiency, but this link is not directly tested here. The study does not manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task (e.g., MPCR, player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.), so its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Götte, L., Gügler, R., & Fehr, E. (2012). The mentalizing network orchestrates the impact of parochial altruism on social norm enforcement. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is strongly modulated by group membership: outgroup members who defect are punished much more than ingroup members who commit the same norm violation. This parochial punishment pattern is supported by increased activation and connectivity in a neural network associated with punishment-related decision processes (right orbitofrontal gyrus, right lateral prefrontal cortex, right dorsal caudatus) for outgroup punishment, and increased activation/connectivity in the mentalizing network (dorsomedial prefrontal cortex, bilateral temporo-parietal junction) for lenient ingroup punishment. The study provides evidence for neural mechanisms underlying parochial altruism in norm enforcement, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option leads to parochial (group-biased) punishment behavior in a third-party punishment context, with outgroup norm violators punished more than ingroup violators. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design is a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with third-party punishment, which is adjacent to but not the same as a repeated public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment can be highly group-biased, and that the behavioral effect of punishment may depend on group context, but the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Apergis-Schoute, A., Herrmann, B., Lieberman, M., Müller, U., Robbins, T. W., & Clark, L. (2013). Serotonin Modulates Striatal Responses to Fairness and Retaliation in Humans. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering serotonin via ATD increases costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the ultimatum game (second-party context), but tends to decrease costly punishment in third-party punishment games. The behavioral effects are accompanied by neural changes: ATD reduces ventral striatal responses to fairness and increases dorsal striatal responses during retaliation. The results suggest that serotonin modulates the drive for retaliation rather than fairness preferences. The games are one-shot, with no repeated interaction, and the main outcomes are behavioral and neural, not payoff-based. There is no direct analysis of efficiency or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and neurobiological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, showing that serotonin depletion increases second-party punishment but decreases third-party punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but rather ultimatum and third-party punishment games. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological drivers of punishment in similar environments, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design features (one-shot, no communication, binary accept/reject, known costs of punishment) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2005). Driving forces behind informal sanctions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a three-player one-shot PD with punishment, cooperators predominantly punish defectors, and this pattern is robust across different punishment effectiveness conditions and elicitation methods. Defectors also punish, but their punishment is more likely to be spiteful and disappears when punishment cannot increase payoff differences. The cost-effectiveness of punishment is manipulated, showing that cooperators are willing to spend more to punish defectors even when punishment is less effective. The results challenge fairness theories that focus only on group-level payoffs or inequality aversion, supporting instead a model where retaliation against unfairness is central. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and motivational aspects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on the behavioral patterns and motives of punishment in a three-player PD with punishment, including how punishment is targeted and how its cost-effectiveness affects behavior. However, it does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is frequently used by cooperators against defectors, and that the willingness to punish persists even when it is costly, which may imply potential for efficiency improvements in similar games, but this is not tested or quantified here. The design details (player count, one-shot, punishment cost/effectiveness) are well specified and can inform mechanistic expectations, but not direct efficiency predictions.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Bernhard, H., Fischbacher, U., & Fehr, E. (2006). Parochial altruism in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly parochial: punishers spend more to punish norm violations when the victim is from their own group, regardless of the violator's group. Dictators (the potential norm violators) expect more punishment and transfer more to the recipient when the recipient and the third party are from the same group. When the third party is from the dictator's group, dictators expect less punishment and transfer less. The results suggest that the threat of punishment is not impartial but is shaped by group membership, leading to greater protection for ingroup victims. The paper does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are not directly about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness and application of punishment in norm enforcement are highly sensitive to group affiliations, with punishment being much more likely and severe when the victim and punisher are from the same group. However, because the experiment is a one-shot dictator game with third-party punishment (not a repeated public goods game), and because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. It is informative about the social and psychological moderators of punishment, especially parochialism, but not about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kurzban, R., DeScioli, P., & O'Brien, E. (2007). Audience effects on moralistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of an audience (even just the experimenter) increases the frequency and amount of third-party punishment of norm violators in both Trust Game and sequential Prisoner's Dilemma Game settings.
