Under anonymous conditions, punishment is infrequent and small; when observed, punishment is more common and larger. The effect is strongest when the audience includes other participants. The study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and does not use a public goods game, but it demonstrates that reputational concerns strongly increase costly punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers informative evidence that the salience of observation (audience effects) can substantially increase the willingness to engage in costly punishment. If a public goods game design includes features that make punishment decisions observable to others, one might expect higher rates of punishment, which could (in other contexts) affect cooperation and possibly efficiency. The lack of efficiency or payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task, but the findings are relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Goette, L., Huffman, D., Meier, S., & Sutter, M. (2012). Competition Between Organizational Groups: Its Impact on Altruistic and Antisocial Motivations. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in a neutral environment, punishment is used prosocially to enforce cooperation, especially when the victim of defection is an in-group member. When competition between groups is introduced, punishment becomes antisocial: out-group members are punished more, regardless of whether they cooperate or defect. Competition increases in-group cooperation but does not reduce out-group cooperation. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the authors argue that antisocial punishment under competition is wasteful and could reduce overall efficiency, while increased in-group cooperation could enhance it. The net effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of intergroup competition can fundamentally change the function of punishment in social dilemma games: from norm enforcement to antisocial, destructive punishment. While the design is not a standard repeated public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting when punishment will be efficiency-enhancing versus efficiency-reducing. Specifically, in environments with salient group competition, enabling punishment may lead to resource-destroying antisocial punishment, undermining the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data means the findings should be used as qualitative guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2016). Looking Under the Hood of Third-Party Punishment Reveals Design for Personal Benefit. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is driven by the punisher's inference that a dictator who mistreats a recipient is also likely to mistreat the punisher. When punishers have information about how they themselves are treated, this information dominates their punishment decisions, and information about third-party treatment is largely ignored. The results support a deterrence-based account of punishment motivation. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motivations for punishment, especially the conditions under which punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not address whether punishment increases or decreases group efficiency. The design is a modified dictator/third-party-punishment game, not a standard public goods game, and no efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Li, J., Xiao, E., Houser, D., & Montague, P. R. (2009). Neural responses to sanction threats in two-party economic exchange. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a two-player repeated trust game, the threat of monetary sanctions (punishment) leads trustees to return significantly less to investors compared to when no sanction is threatened. This effect is robust across fair and unfair investor requests. Neural data show that the presence of a sanction threat reduces activation in brain regions associated with social reward (VMPFC, LOFC, amygdala) and increases activation in regions associated with rational, utility-based decision making (parietal cortex). The findings support the 'perception shift' hypothesis: sanction threats crowd out norm-based motivations and shift behavior toward self-interested, utility-maximizing choices. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, but the reduction in reciprocity implies a likely reduction in efficiency relative to the no-sanction condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player repeated trust game, the introduction of a punishment (sanction) threat reduces cooperative behavior (reciprocity) by trustees. While the design is not a standard public goods game, the mechanism—punishment crowding out intrinsic motivation—may generalize to PGG-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment can reduce efficiency or group payoff in environments where cooperation is norm-driven and where punishment is perceived as a signal of distrust or as crowding out intrinsic motivation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total earnings, the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the reduction in reciprocity. The findings are most relevant for two-player, repeated, sequential games with optional punishment, and less directly for standard multi-player PGGs.

- source: Cameron, L., Chaudhuri, A., Erkal, N., & Gangadharan, L. (2009). Propensities to engage in and punish corrupt behavior: Experimental evidence from Australia, India, Indonesia and Singapore. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish corrupt behavior varies more across cultures than the propensity to engage in corruption. Indian subjects are most likely to offer and accept bribes and least likely to punish, while Indonesian subjects are least likely to engage in corruption and most likely to punish. The manipulation of the bribe's welfare impact (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) only significantly affects behavior in Australia, where higher harm reduces punishment rates and increases bribery. In other countries, this manipulation has little effect. The main outcomes are behavioral (bribery and punishment rates/amounts), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how punishment opportunities and the cost/harm structure of norm violations affect the willingness to punish and engage in norm-violating behavior in a corruption context. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a three-player, one-shot, sequential-move game with endogenous costly punishment, but not a repeated or group-based public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment rates and their deterrent effect are highly culture-dependent and may not always translate into higher efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and the harm from norm violation is high. The manipulation of the harm from corruption (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) shows that increasing the harm can sometimes reduce punishment and increase norm violation, due to the increased cost to the victim. This is relevant for predicting when punishment will be effective in improving efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Delton, A. W., & Krasnow, M. M. (2017). The psychology of deterrence explains why group membership matters for third-party punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly influenced by group membership: punishers assign the most punishment when an outgroup dictator treats an ingroup recipient poorly, and the least when an ingroup dictator treats an outgroup recipient poorly. This pattern is best explained by deterrence psychology, where punishers infer that outgroup dictators who mistreat ingroup members are likely to mistreat the punisher as well. The group norm maintenance theory (fairness or parochialism norms) does not fit the observed punishment patterns. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and inferences about personal valuation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological mechanisms underlying third-party punishment and how group membership moderates punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most useful for understanding when and why punishment is likely to be assigned, especially in relation to group membership, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Shinada, M., & Yamagishia, T. (2007). Punishing free riders: direct and indirect promotion of cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that third-party punishment is group-based: cooperators in the gift-giving game punish in-group cheaters more severely than out-group cheaters, while noncooperators show the opposite pattern. Most punishment is directed at the least cooperative group members. Emotional responses (anger, unfairness, guilt) are correlated with punishment of in-group cheaters but not out-group cheaters. The design uses a gift-giving game with a public-goods-like structure, but does not directly measure or report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral allocation of punishment in group-structured social exchange games, especially regarding in-group vs. out-group targeting. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design and findings are most useful for understanding the social and motivational determinants of punishment, not its impact on group welfare or efficiency. The punishment mechanism (costly to punisher, 3x impact on target) and the absence of feedback or repeated interaction are relevant for mapping to other designs, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Cosmides, L., Pedersen, E. J., & Tooby, J. (2012). What Are Punishment and Reputation for?. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that (1) subjects use third-party reputation to regulate trust only when first-party (personal) information is unavailable; (2) punishment is not more likely to be directed at partners with a reputation for norm violation; (3) subjects are more likely to cooperate with defectors whom they have punished, consistent with punishment as a bargaining tool for future cooperation; (4) when deciding to cooperate, subjects rely on the partner's most recent behavior toward themselves, not on past reputation. The results support social exchange theory over group norm maintenance theory in the context of dyadic trust games with punishment. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological function of punishment in dyadic trust games, showing that punishment is used to bargain for better treatment rather than to enforce group norms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player setting. The findings suggest that in small-scale, dyadic games, the presence of punishment may not increase group efficiency, but rather serves to regulate individual relationships. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether the game context is dyadic or group-based, and on the psychological mechanisms engaged. The design dimensions most relevant here are the dyadic structure, the use of reputation, and the role of punishment as a bargaining tool.

- source: Henrich, J., McElreath, R., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., Marlowe, F. W., Tracer, D., & Ziker, J. (2006). Costly punishment across human societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that costly punishment (both second-party and third-party) is a widespread human behavior, but its prevalence and intensity vary substantially across societies. In all populations, more unequal offers are punished more frequently, but the threshold for punishment and the willingness to punish differ. There is a strong positive correlation between a population's willingness to punish and its average altruistic behavior (as measured by dictator game offers). The findings support theories of gene-culture coevolution in human cooperation. However, the paper does not use public goods games and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is a robust and variable feature of human behavior across societies, and that it is positively associated with altruism. However, because the experiments are ultimatum and third-party punishment games (not public goods games), and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that the effect of punishment on cooperation-related behaviors is likely to be highly variable across populations, and that willingness to punish is linked to local norms of altruism. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in repeated or group-based public goods settings.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2005). Emotion expression in human punishment behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when responders in the ultimatum game are allowed to send a message to proposers expressing their feelings about the offer, the rejection rate of unfair offers (≤20% to responder) drops from 60% (no emotion expression allowed) to 32% (emotion expression allowed). This effect is statistically significant. The ability to express negative emotions through messages appears to reduce the use of costly punishment. The distribution of offers by proposers does not differ between treatments, indicating that the effect is due to responder behavior. The study does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, but the behavioral change would mechanically increase total earnings by reducing the frequency of zero-payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in environments where costly punishment is possible, introducing a non-costly channel for emotion expression can reduce the use of costly punishment. While the study is not a public goods game, it is highly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment and the potential for communication or emotion expression to moderate punishment behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling communication or emotion expression may reduce the negative efficiency impact of punishment by reducing its frequency. However, the findings are specific to the ultimatum game and may not transfer directly to public goods games without further evidence.

- source: Yudkin, D. A., Rothmund, T., Twardawski, M., Thalla, N., & Van Bavel, J. J. (2016). Reflexive Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned to a third-party role punished out-group transgressors more harshly than in-group transgressors, especially under conditions promoting reflexive judgment (fast response or cognitive load). The effect is robust across different group identity manipulations (sports teams, nationality). The punishment is costless to the punisher and only removes money from the transgressor. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The findings are about behavioral bias in punishment assignment, not about the impact of punishment on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological determinants of punishment assignment (intergroup bias, reflexivity) in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on cooperation. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games but is a one-shot, three-player 'stealing and punishment' scenario with costless punishment. Thus, the findings are not directly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. The main relevance is for understanding when and how punishment is assigned, not its effect on group welfare.

- source: de Quervain, D. J. F., Fischbacher, U., Treyer, V., Schelthammer, M., Schnyder, U., Buck, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). The neural basis of altruistic punishment. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants are given the opportunity to punish defectors in a trust game, they frequently do so, even at a personal cost. Effective (costly) punishment, as opposed to symbolic (non-impactful) punishment, is associated with increased activation in the dorsal striatum (caudate nucleus), a brain region implicated in reward processing. The amount of money spent on punishment correlates with the level of caudate activation, suggesting that anticipated satisfaction motivates punishment. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment compared to costless punishment, but does not eliminate it. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, but does show that punishment is used and is psychologically rewarding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, individuals are willing to pay to punish defectors, and that this behavior is motivated by anticipated satisfaction. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total welfare, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a trust game with a punishment stage. The findings support the idea that costly punishment will be used when available, but do not provide direct evidence about whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency/intensity, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barr, A., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Ensminger, J., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, J., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., McElreath, R., & Tracer, D. (2008). More 'altruistic' punishment in larger societies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the level of third-party punishment (as measured by MAO) is significantly higher in larger and more complex societies. Both local group size and ethnic group size are strong predictors of the willingness to punish unfair offers in a third-party punishment game. The results suggest that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, possibly as a response to increased anonymity and collective action problems. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the relationship between group size and the prevalence of third-party punishment in dictator-like games, which are adjacent to public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that the willingness to punish norm violations increases with group size and social complexity, which may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment is more likely to be used or effective. For the prediction task, this paper is mainly informative about the social and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Wang, C. S., Sivanathan, N., Narayanan, J., Ganegoda, D. B., Bauer, M., Bodenhausen, G. V., & Murnighan, K. (2011). Retribution and emotional regulation: The effects of time delay in angry economic interactions. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across five lab experiments, the authors show that introducing a time delay before making punishment decisions (e.g., rejecting unfair offers or punishing norm violators) generally reduces costly punishment, but only if the delay is accompanied by distraction or cognitive reappraisal. If participants ruminate during the delay, punishment increases. The effect is mediated by negative emotions (anger). The experiments use one-shot or single-decision ultimatum games and third-party punishment trust games, with punishment measured as rejection rates or points spent. No direct group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, but the reduction in costly punishment implies less wasteful destruction of resources. The findings highlight the importance of emotional regulation in moderating punitive impulses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the context and psychological state of punishers (e.g., time delay, distraction, rumination) can strongly moderate the frequency and magnitude of costly punishment. While the games are not public goods games, the results suggest that interventions reducing negative emotion or promoting reappraisal/distraction could reduce inefficient punishment and thus improve group payoffs. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, and the games are not repeated or multi-player PGGs, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence for prediction tasks.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A. (2008). The price you pay: cost-dependent reputation effects of altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who invest more in costly third-party punishment are perceived as more fair and trustworthy, and are subsequently entrusted with more money in a trust game. This effect is contingent on the cost being a reliable signal (i.e., known relative to endowment). The effect is mediated by perceptions of fairness, not by general likability or generosity. The study does not measure group efficiency or public goods provision, but rather the reputational and trust consequences for punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide indirect evidence that costly punishment can enhance the punisher's reputation and increase trust from others, but only when the cost is interpretable as a reliable signal. For prediction tasks focused on group efficiency in PGGs, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that punishment may have reputational benefits for the punisher, which could, in theory, affect cooperation and efficiency in repeated or reputation-sensitive environments, but it does not test this directly. The design and outcomes are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Perugini, M., Gallucci, M., Presaghi, F., & Ercolani, A. P. (2003). The personal norm of reciprocity. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that individual differences in positive and negative reciprocity (as measured by the PNR questionnaire) predict how people allocate money to others in response to fair or unfair prior actions, both in hypothetical and incentivized settings. Positive reciprocity predicts greater rewarding of positive behavior, while negative reciprocity predicts greater punishment of negative behavior. These effects are robust across cultures (Italy and UK) and are distinct from general prosocial orientation or Big Five personality traits. However, the studies do not directly examine group efficiency, total welfare, or the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes in repeated public goods or social dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in the propensity to punish or reward (reciprocity norms) influence how people respond to others' actions in allocation games, including costly punishment and reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings are most useful for understanding heterogeneity in punishment/reward behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. The allocation tasks are adjacent to PGGs but lack repeated group interaction, explicit MPCR, or group efficiency measures.

- source: Ruff, C. C., Ugazio, G., & Fehr, E. (2013). Changing Social Norm Compliance with Noninvasive Brain Stimulation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the threat of punishment increases norm compliance (higher transfers) in a modified dictator game, and that noninvasive brain stimulation of the rLPFC can causally modulate this effect. Anodal tDCS (increasing excitability) increases sanction-induced compliance (larger transfer difference between punishment and baseline rounds), while cathodal tDCS (decreasing excitability) reduces it. The effect is stronger in social (human) punishment contexts than in non-social (computer) contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral compliance rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment opportunities increases norm compliance in a dictator-like game, and that this effect is modulated by neural mechanisms. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The behavioral results (increased compliance under punishment) are consistent with the broader literature, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2011). Punishment sustains large-scale cooperation in prestate warfare. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The Turkana sustain costly, large-scale cooperation in warfare through informal punishment of free riders. Raiding parties are large and composed of unrelated individuals from multiple social units. Free riding (desertion, cowardice, unfair loot appropriation) is common, but is often sanctioned through verbal reprimands, corporal punishment, and fines. The system relies on third-party consensus and community enforcement, not formal institutions. Norms governing warfare are group-beneficial at the ethnolinguistic scale, not just for smaller social units. While the paper provides rich evidence on the mechanisms and scale of cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct measures of efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the real-world operation of punishment and its role in sustaining cooperation at large scales, especially in the absence of formal institutions. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a controlled experimental design. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, the paper offers strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation and reduce free riding, even in large, anonymous groups. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, rather than for parameterizing efficiency outcomes in laboratory PGGs. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective at large group sizes and in the absence of repeated interaction or kinship, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency changes due to punishment in a controlled game setting.

- source: Balafoutas, L., & Nikiforakis, N. (2012). Norm enforcement in the city: A natural field experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that some individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment to enforce social norms in one-shot, anonymous field interactions, but this is rare (11.7% overall, 4% for non-littering, 19.3% for escalator norm). Men are more likely to punish than women; the violator's height and gender do not significantly affect enforcement rates. The main reason for not punishing is fear of retaliation. The better-known (universal) norm (non-littering) is enforced less often than the environment-specific norm (escalator), despite being more widely valued. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the willingness to engage in costly punishment in real-world, one-shot norm violation settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings suggest that, in natural field settings, the rate of costly punishment is much lower than in laboratory public goods games, possibly due to higher perceived costs (e.g., risk of retaliation) and lack of anonymity. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the mere availability of punishment may not translate into high rates of enforcement or efficiency gains in field-like conditions. However, because no group payoff or efficiency data are reported, the paper cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the behavioral limits of punishment in naturalistic settings and the importance of context (e.g., fear of counter-punishment, norm universality) as moderators.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2014). The cost of cowardice: punitive sentiments towards free riders in Turkana raids. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana participants are significantly more likely to express punitive sentiments (both direct and indirect) toward hypothetical warriors who free ride (display cowardice) in combat, compared to those who fail to contribute due to inability (lack of skill). Direct sanctions include criticism and corporal punishment, while indirect sanctions include withholding social support, lending, and marriage opportunities. The findings support the idea that both direct punishment and indirect reciprocity are important mechanisms for sustaining large-scale cooperation in acephalous societies. However, the study does not measure actual payoffs, group efficiency, or the effect of punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punitive motivations toward free riders are present in a real-world, high-stakes collective action context, and that both direct and indirect sanctions are socially endorsed. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a controlled game, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The findings are most useful for understanding the psychological and social underpinnings of punishment in collective action, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Price, M. E., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2002). Punitive sentiment as an anti-free rider psychological device. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals' willingness to participate in a collective action (e.g., military draft) is a strong predictor of their desire to punish nonparticipants (free riders), but not of their desire to reward participants. Perceived self-interest in the group goal predicts pro-reward sentiment but not punitive sentiment, once willingness to participate is controlled for. The results suggest that punitive sentiment is a specialized psychological adaptation for eliminating the fitness advantage of free riders, rather than for recruiting labor or maximizing group efficiency. The study does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a public goods game or similar environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its findings are based on survey responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or payoffs. However, it does provide indirect support for the idea that punitive sentiment is psychologically linked to participation in collective action, and that punishment may function to deter free riding. For the prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Patil, I., & Cushman, F. (2021). Reputational and cooperative benefits of third-party compensation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that third-party compensation leads to greater reputational and partner choice benefits than third-party punishment, as judged by observers in hypothetical scenarios and vignettes. Compensators are seen as more moral, trustworthy, and are more often chosen as partners in economic games (Trust Game, Dictator Game). These effects are robust across different contexts, including workplace and real-life vignettes. The paper also finds that compensators tend to have lower Dark Triad personality traits. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not provide evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or third-party punishment games. Its findings are relevant for understanding reputational and partner choice consequences of punishment versus compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the hypothetical games, but the dependent variables are not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). Direct and indirect punishment among strangers in the field. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that (1) direct punishment of norm violators is not rewarded by third parties in one-shot field interactions; (2) indirect punishment, operationalized as withholding help from a norm violator, is common and significantly reduces the likelihood that violators receive help; (3) the availability of indirect punishment opportunities crowds out the use of direct punishment; and (4) there are notable sex differences in the application of direct and indirect punishment. The experiment does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides evidence on the behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement in natural settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its main contribution is to show, in a natural field setting, that indirect punishment (withholding help) is more common and preferred over direct punishment when both are available, and that direct punishment is not socially rewarded. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where indirect punishment is possible and not costly, direct costly punishment may be less prevalent, but the paper does not inform how this affects group efficiency or payoff. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as the norm violation (littering) is a negative externality, but there is no explicit group payoff or contribution structure. Thus, the paper is more informative about behavioral mechanisms than about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Brethel-Haurwitz, K. M., Stoycos, S. A., Cardinale, E. M., Huebner, B., & Marsh, A. A. (2016). Is costly punishment altruistic? Exploring rejection of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game in real-world altruists. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that extraordinary real-world altruists (altruistic kidney donors) do not engage in more costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the Ultimatum Game than controls. However, individuals with higher self-reported normative altruism (SRA) are more likely to reject unfair offers, indicating that costly punishment is associated with norm sensitivity rather than altruism per se. The study provides evidence that costly punishment in the UG is better understood as norm-enforcing behavior rather than altruistic behavior. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is conducted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motivations behind costly punishment in the UG, suggesting that such punishment is driven by norm enforcement rather than altruism. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, as it does not report or analyze payoff-based outcomes. However, it does reinforce that the presence of punishment in UG-like games is not necessarily linked to increased altruism or prosociality, and that behavioral outcomes (like rejection rates) may not translate to efficiency gains.

- source: Eriksson, K., Stringing, P., Andersson, P. A., & Lindholm, T. (2017). Costly punishment in the ultimatum game evokes moral concern, in particular when framed as payoff reduction. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across a series of online experiments, the authors find that framing costly punishment as 'reduction' (explicitly paying to reduce the other's payoff) leads to less frequent use of punishment, greater moral concern about using punishment, and a stronger association with punitive motives, compared to the standard 'rejection' framing in the ultimatum game. The reduction frame is more likely to be seen as immoral and as punishment, while rejection is more often seen as a quest for fairness. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are limited to the ultimatum game, which is structurally adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The main outcomes are behavioral (frequency of punishment, motives, moral judgments), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, except as a caution that framing and perceived morality can influence the use of punishment. It does, however, highlight that the way punishment is framed can substantially affect its use, which may be relevant for interpreting behavioral outcomes in other game contexts.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Liebe, U. (2016). Generosity is a sign of trustworthiness-the punishment of selfishness is not. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals who punish selfishness in a dictator game are not more trustworthy nor more trusted in a subsequent trust game, regardless of whether punishment is second-party or third-party. In fact, second-party punishers are trusted less than non-punishers. Generosity in the dictator game, however, is a strong predictor of trustworthiness and is perceived as such by others. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and belief-based measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it uses a binary dictator game with punishment and a trust game, and reports only on behavioral outcomes (punishment, generosity, trust, trustworthiness, beliefs). The findings suggest that punishment, at least in this context, does not enhance trust or trustworthiness, and by implication may not improve efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The design and results may be informative for understanding the signaling value of punishment versus generosity, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs.

- source: O'Gorman, R., Wilson, D. S., & Miller, R. R. (2005). Altruistic punishing and helping differ in sensitivity to relatedness, friendship, and future interactions. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that participants report a strong desire to punish cheaters at personal cost, regardless of whether the cheater is a relative, friend, or stranger, and regardless of the likelihood of future interaction. In contrast, willingness to help is much more sensitive to relatedness and future interaction. The study does not use real economic games or measure group efficiency or payoffs, but rather relies on self-reported hypothetical willingness to punish or help. The results suggest that the psychological impulse to punish norm violators is less conditional on kinship or reciprocity cues than the impulse to help, at least in hypothetical settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that the psychological willingness to punish is robust to changes in relatedness and future interaction cues, which may imply that, in real games, punishment behavior could be less sensitive to these variables than helping behavior. However, because the study does not use real payoffs, group outcomes, or actual public goods games, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited and indirect.

- source: White, S. F., Brislin, S. J., Sinclair, S., & Blair, J. R. (2014). Punishing unfairness: Rewarding or the organization of a reactively aggressive response?. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that as participants punish unfair offers more severely, there is increased activation in brain regions associated with reactive aggression (PAG, dmPFC, AIC) and decreased activation in reward-related regions (vmPFC, PCC). The behavioral data show that participants spend more to punish as offers become more unfair. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings challenge the view that punishment in social exchange games is motivated by reward, instead supporting a reactive aggression interpretation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social exchange games. It is relevant for understanding the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior, but does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in informing mechanistic or motivational models of punishment, not payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Molho, C., Tybur, J. M., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2020). Direct and indirect punishment of norm violations in daily life. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that people frequently use both direct and indirect punishment strategies in response to norm violations in daily life. Direct confrontation is more likely when the punisher has been personally victimized, has more power, or values the offender more. Indirect punishment (gossip, social exclusion) is more likely when the violation is severe, the punisher has less power, or values the offender less. Emotional responses (anger, disgust) also differentially predict punishment type. The findings support the idea that punishment is context-sensitive and motivated by both the benefits of changing others' behavior and the risks of retaliation. However, the study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and contextual determinants of punishment strategy in naturalistic settings, but not for quantifying the impact of punishment on efficiency. The study may be useful for mechanism discussions or for understanding when punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., & McCullough, M. E. (2018). The Unresponsive Avenger: More Evidence That Disinterested Third Parties Do Not Punish Altruistically. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across five lab experiments, participants who were directly harmed (insulted) by another person reliably punished the insulter using an aversive sound blast. Participants who witnessed a friend being insulted also punished the insulter, but less strongly. However, participants who witnessed a stranger being insulted generally did not punish the insulter more than they punished uninvolved bystanders, except in one experiment where social censure may have been a factor. The results suggest that third-party punishment is not a robust, general tendency, but is contingent on welfare interdependence (e.g., self or friends). The findings cast doubt on the external validity of economic game results that report high rates of third-party punishment, suggesting those may be inflated by experimental demand. No efficiency or payoff-based outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is to question the generalizability of third-party punishment effects observed in economic games, suggesting that such effects may be overestimated due to experimental demand. For the downstream prediction task, this paper signals that the presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee increased norm enforcement or efficiency, especially when punishment is not personally relevant or when welfare interdependence is low. However, because no efficiency or payoff outcomes are measured, the paper cannot inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, L., & Murnighan, J. K. (2017). The Dynamics of Punishment and Trust. *Journal of Applied Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that punishment increases observers' trust in the punisher when the punished individual is seen as deserving, but decreases trust when the punishment is undeserved or too severe. Medium or large punishment for deserving targets increases trust, but large punishment for less deserving targets decreases trust. Punishers who are perceived as acting out of self-interest (e.g., who benefit from the punishment) are trusted less than altruistic punishers. These effects are robust across scenario-based and behavioral lab studies. However, the paper does not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all dependent variables are trust-related or perception-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma games with or without punishment. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment affects trust in the punisher, especially as a function of deservingness, punishment magnitude, and punisher motives. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from game design plus control efficiency), this paper offers no direct evidence, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational effects of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Winter, F., & Zhang, N. (2018). Social norm enforcement in ethnically diverse communities. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds strong ethnic asymmetries in social norm enforcement: native Germans are more likely to sanction norm violations, and ethnic minorities are more likely to be the targets of sanctions. These patterns are interpreted as resulting from status inequalities between groups. The aggregate probability of being sanctioned for a norm violation (social control) increases with the share of minorities in the population, but this is driven by natives sanctioning minorities at higher rates. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game or direct variant, but is adjacent in that it studies real-world norm enforcement and punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about real-world punishment (sanctioning) behavior in response to norm violations, with a focus on ethnic asymmetries. It does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. However, it may be useful for understanding how social context (e.g., group identity, status, diversity) can moderate the frequency and direction of punishment, which could be relevant for interpreting or designing PGG experiments with diverse populations. The lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: McCabe, K. A., Rassenti, S. J., & Smith, V. L. (1996). Game theory and reciprocity in some extensive form experimental games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that allowing for direct punishment of defection (game 1) leads to higher cooperation and efficiency compared to when punishment is not available (game 2), though the difference is modest in single-play but more pronounced in repeated interactions. Efficiency (as a percentage of the cooperative joint payoff) is highest in repeated same-pair treatments with punishment (90.6%), and lower in random or single-play treatments (82.6%-86.9%). The presence of punishment increases the expected payoff for cooperative choices and reduces the profitability of defection. The probability of cooperation increases with the likelihood of repeated interaction with the same partner, and punishment is used at nontrivial rates even when costly. Overall, punishment and repeated interaction both have positive effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in extensive form bargaining games with a punishment option, enabling punishment increases efficiency, especially when combined with repeated interaction with the same partner. The efficiency boost from punishment is present even in single-play, but is much larger in repeated games. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence of punishment, the matching protocol (same partner vs random), and the number of rounds. The efficiency values reported (Table 4) can be used as quantitative anchors for similar game designs. The findings support the prediction that, controlling for baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the probability of repeated interaction and the cost/effectiveness of punishment.

- source: Mendoza, S. A., Lane, S. P., & Amodio, D. M. (2014). For Members Only: Ingroup Punishment of Fairness Norm Violations in the Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in the ultimatum game, participants are more likely to reject marginally unfair offers from in-group members than from out-group members, even though rejection is costly to both parties. This pattern is interpreted as stricter enforcement of fairness norms within groups. The effect is most pronounced for offers that are only slightly unfair (e.g., $8/$20), and is stronger among participants with higher in-group identification. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, and the outcome is measured in terms of behavioral responses (accept/reject) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and social moderators of costly punishment in a bargaining context (ultimatum game), specifically the role of group membership and social identity. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment is more likely to be used (e.g., in-group contexts, marginal norm violations), but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Enke, B. (2019). KINSHIP, COOPERATION, AND THE EVOLUTION OF MORAL SYSTEMS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that kinship structure predicts the type of moral system a society develops: tight kinship societies rely on communal values, shame, revenge, and local institutions, while loose kinship societies develop universal values, guilt, impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment, and large-scale institutions. These systems are internally consistent and mediate cooperation patterns, such as in-group favoritism and trust. Theoretical modeling suggests that universalizing moral systems in loose kinship societies enable more efficient cooperation with strangers, which becomes advantageous with economic development. However, empirical analyses focus on psychological, cultural, and institutional variables, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of punishment and moral systems in supporting cooperation, especially in relation to kinship structure and the scope of cooperation. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments. However, it suggests that impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment is more prevalent in societies with loose kinship ties and universal values, which are theoretically linked to higher efficiency in broad cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how cultural and institutional context may moderate the effectiveness of punishment, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects in controlled game settings.

- source: Gurven, M., & Winking, J. (2008). Collective action in action: Prosocial behavior in and out of the laboratory. *American Anthropologist*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that among the Tsimane, offers in the Dictator Game, Ultimatum Game, and Third-Party Punishment Game are low, and punishment (rejection or third-party punishment) is infrequent. There is little to no correlation between individuals' behavior in these games and their real-world prosocial actions such as food sharing, labor for public goods (well construction), or contributions to communal feasts. The results suggest that context and payoff structure are more important than stable individual traits in predicting prosocial behavior. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and thus does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it includes a third-party punishment game (TPPG), the outcome measures are individual offers and punishment rates, not group efficiency or payoff. The study does show that punishment is rare and offers are low in this context, suggesting that, at least in this population and game structure, punishment does not play a strong role in promoting prosocial behavior. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper cannot be used to calibrate or predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

A., Tsirbas, Y., Wan, R., Widodo, S., Zein, R., Zhang, Q. P., Zirganou-Kazolea, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Perceptions of the appropriate response to norm violation in 57 societies. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that across cultures, the perceived appropriateness of informal sanctions (confrontation, gossip, ostracism) increases as the perceived inappropriateness of a norm violation increases. There is substantial cross-country variation in which sanctions are viewed as most appropriate, and this variation is consistent across norm violation domains (including a common resource depletion scenario). Metanorms for different sanctions are not interchangeable: for example, societies that view physical confrontation as less appropriate tend to view gossip as more appropriate, and vice versa. The perceived appropriateness of sanctions correlates with cultural, economic, and ecological variables (e.g., higher income countries view confrontation as less appropriate and gossip as more appropriate). However, the study does not measure actual cooperation, punishment behavior, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report on actual game play, contributions, or payoffs, nor does it manipulate punishment availability or cost. Its main relevance is contextual: it shows that beliefs about the appropriateness of punishment and other sanctions vary widely across societies, even for cooperation-related norm violations. This may inform expectations about the acceptability or uptake of punishment in different cultural contexts, but does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Li, L. F., & Xiao, E. (2014). Money Talks: Rebate Mechanisms in Reputation System Design. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when feedback reporting is costly, market efficiency (proportion of efficient trades and participant earnings) declines. Introducing a rebate mechanism, where sellers can offer a partial rebate to buyers for leaving feedback, increases efficiency—especially when sellers frequently offer rebates. The rebate acts as a signal of seller cooperativeness, increasing buyers' willingness to purchase and sellers' willingness to ship. The effect is robust even when the rebate does not fully cover the reporting cost. The more often sellers offer rebates, the higher the efficiency and earnings for both buyers and sellers. If sellers do not offer rebates, efficiency can be even lower than in the no-rebate, high-cost condition. Over time, sellers learn to offer rebates more frequently, further improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a mechanism for sellers to precommit to (partially) covering buyers' feedback costs increases efficiency in a repeated trust game with feedback, especially when sellers frequently use the mechanism. The effect is strongest when the rebate is offered often, and the mechanism works even if the rebate is less than the full reporting cost. For prediction, if a game design allows for a precommitment rebate mechanism and sellers are likely to use it, efficiency should be higher than in a comparable game with high feedback costs and no rebate. The control efficiency (with high feedback cost and no rebate) is a lower bound; the treatment efficiency (with rebate) will be higher, approaching the efficiency of the low-cost or automatic feedback conditions as rebate frequency increases. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/reputation games with feedback as a public good, and less directly to standard PGGs or punishment games, but the mechanism is analogous to reward/precommitment interventions in PGGs.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2016). Efficient Coordination in Weakest-Link Games. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated weakest-link games, groups with fixed interaction structure (all interact with all) rapidly converge to inefficient equilibria, especially as group size increases. In contrast, when players can choose their interaction neighbourhoods (i.e., whom to interact with), groups quickly achieve and sustain near-optimal efficiency and welfare, even in groups of 24. The key mechanism is that high-effort players exclude low-effort players, who then increase their effort to regain inclusion. This exclusion is often costly to both parties, resembling costly punishment, but the effect persists even when exclusion is not materially costly (i.e., in a 'self-protection' treatment). The results are robust to severe reductions in information, alternative payoff functions, and large group sizes. The findings suggest that enabling endogenous partner choice is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient coordination in weakest-link environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous neighbourhood choice (partner selection) in weakest-link games leads to large, robust increases in group efficiency and welfare, compared to fixed-group baselines. The effect is observed across a range of group sizes (8 and 24), information conditions, and payoff structures. The mechanism is exclusion of low-effort members, which induces higher effort and thus higher group payoffs. Importantly, the effect does not depend on exclusion being materially costly (punishment per se is not required). For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of endogenous partner choice is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of exclusion/punishment-like mechanisms in weakest-link (and possibly related public goods) environments. However, the results are for weakest-link (minimum effort) games, which are adjacent but not identical to standard linear public goods games.

- source: Fudenberg, D., Rand, D. G., & Dreber, A. (2012). Slow to Anger and Fast to Forgive: Cooperation in an Uncertain World. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with noise, cooperation is frequent and yields higher payoffs than defection when the benefit/cost ratio is high enough to support cooperative equilibria. Lenient and forgiving strategies (e.g., Tit-for-2-Tats, Grim2) are both common and successful in these settings. When only defection is an equilibrium (low b/c), defection dominates and is most profitable. The presence of noise increases the use of longer-memory, more lenient strategies. The paper provides detailed payoff matrices and shows that, in treatments with cooperative equilibria, the highest-earning strategies are lenient and forgiving. Social preferences (as measured by a dictator game) do not explain cooperation; strategic concerns do.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with and without noise, especially as a function of the benefit/cost ratio and error rate. While not a public goods game, the repeated PD is closely related, and the findings on how game structure (payoff ratios, noise) affect the efficiency of cooperative vs. noncooperative strategies are directly relevant. The paper provides explicit expected payoffs for each strategy under different conditions, allowing for quantitative prediction of group efficiency. The results show that, when the game supports cooperative equilibria, efficiency is higher and cooperative strategies outperform defectors. The absence of explicit punishment/reward mechanisms means the findings are most relevant for environments where punishment is endogenous (via strategy choice) rather than via a separate punishment stage.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2004). Promises, threats and fairness. *Economic Journal*.
