  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a one-shot investment/bargaining game, allowing pre-play communication (either a promise from the buyer or a threat from the seller) increases the rate of investment and the mean profit of the investor (seller) compared to no communication. Buyer promises are always kept and lead to the highest investor profits (mean profit +8.75 SEK), while seller threats are less credible and less effective (mean profit -6.43 SEK). Without communication, mean profit is lowest (-12.86 SEK). The study provides direct evidence that communication, especially credible promises, can mitigate efficiency losses in social dilemmas similar to public goods or hold-up problems, even in the absence of formal punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling credible communication (especially promises) between parties in a social dilemma increases efficiency and group payoff, even in a one-shot setting. While the game is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related in structure (investment creates surplus, division is bargained). The findings suggest that, in similar environments, introducing a channel for credible promises (but not necessarily punishment) can substantially improve efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting the effect of communication or commitment devices, rather than punishment per se, but the analysis of threats (costly punishment) is informative: threats are less credible and less effective than promises. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that communication/commitment channels can substitute for formal punishment in improving efficiency, but that the effect depends on the credibility and nature of the commitment (promise vs. threat).

- source: Brown, M., Falk, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). Relational contracts and the nature of market interactions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The absence of third-party enforcement leads to the endogenous formation of long-term firm-worker relationships, where contract renewal is contingent on high effort. This informal punishment mechanism (firing/termination) disciplines workers and results in higher effort, higher wages, and much higher efficiency and surplus compared to settings where such relationships are not possible. The effect is robust: both firms and workers earn more in long-term relationships, and the distribution of surplus is more equal. When long-term relationships are ruled out (random rematching), effort and efficiency drop sharply, and minimal effort becomes common. When third-party enforcement is available, the market behaves competitively, with low wages, low rent sharing, and little incentive for long-term relationships. The findings show that informal, endogenous punishment (via contract renewal/firing) can substitute for formal enforcement and substantially improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated market-like environments with incomplete contracts, the ability to endogenously form long-term relationships and use contract renewal/firing as a sanction leads to much higher efficiency and surplus than settings where such informal punishment is not possible. For prediction tasks, the presence of repeated interactions, the ability to condition future play on past behavior, and the absence of third-party enforcement are key moderators of punishment's positive effect on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where informal punishment via relationship termination is feasible, efficiency can approach the level seen with formal enforcement, provided that agents can build reputations and relationships. The results are most directly relevant to labor market and relational contract settings, but the underlying mechanism is adjacent to public goods games with endogenous partner choice and repeated play.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Barclay, P. (2016). Exploring the trade-off between quality and fairness in human partner choice. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: When given a choice between a rich-stingy and a poor-fair partner in a modified Dictator Game, choosers often select the fair partner, even at a cost to their own expected pay-off. This tendency is stronger when the wealth of partners is unstable. When both partners are equally fair or unfair, choosers prefer the richer partner, aligning with maximizing expected pay-off. The findings indicate that fairness is prioritized over ability (wealth) in partner choice, especially under uncertainty, and that individuals are willing to incur personal costs to avoid rewarding unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where individuals can choose partners, people may sacrifice efficiency (personal or group payoff) to avoid unfair partners, especially when partner quality is unstable. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting that the introduction of partner choice or similar sanctioning mechanisms may not always increase efficiency—if individuals use these mechanisms to punish unfairness even at a cost to themselves. The results suggest that the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency may be less positive or even negative when individuals are motivated by fairness rather than payoff maximization.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schneider, F. (2010). Eyes are on us, but nobody cares: are eye cues relevant for strong reciprocity?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that subtle, implicit reputation cues (eyespots) do not increase trustees' altruistic behavior in a trust game, nor do they affect trustors' willingness to invest. In contrast, explicit pecuniary reputation incentives—where trustees' past back-transfers are revealed to future trustors—lead to a large and significant increase in both trustees' back-transfers and trustors' investments. This effect is particularly pronounced among individuals with high Machiavellianism scores. The results suggest that explicit, material reputation incentives are powerful drivers of prosocial behavior, while implicit cues without real consequences are ineffective in this context. The findings cast doubt on the hypothesis that most prosocial behavior in anonymous experiments is due to uncontrolled implicit reputation cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that explicit, material reputation incentives (a form of positive sanctioning or reward) can substantially increase prosocial behavior and, by implication, group efficiency in trust-based economic games. However, it also shows that implicit cues (such as eyespots) have no such effect. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of explicit reputation or reward mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency, while subtle cues without real payoff consequences are not. The results are adjacent to public goods games: the trust game is a two-player, sequential, one-shot game repeated with random rematching, not a standard PGG, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or reward is at stake, and less so for punishment-based designs. The paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, but supports the importance of explicit incentive structures for efficiency.

- source: Wang, J., Suri, S., & Watts, D. J. (2012). Cooperation and assortativity with dynamic partner updating. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that dynamic partner updating—where players can choose to make or break ties with others—substantially increases both cooperation rates and average payoffs in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, compared to static networks. Even low rates of partner updating are sufficient to produce large positive effects. The effect is concave: initial increases in update rate have large effects, but further increases yield smaller gains. When the payoff matrix is modified so that maintaining ties with defectors is more costly, cooperation is even higher and more sustained, as players are more willing to sever ties with defectors. The main mechanism is assortative mixing: cooperators preferentially connect with other cooperators and avoid defectors, rather than punishing defectors directly. The results suggest that dynamic partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment or reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation and increasing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling dynamic partner updating (i.e., endogenous, bilateral partner choice and unilateral link deletion) in repeated social dilemma games can dramatically increase group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum), even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The effect is robust across a range of update rates and is further enhanced when the cost of interacting with defectors is increased. For prediction tasks, the presence of dynamic partner updating should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially when players can freely choose partners and sever ties. However, the game is an iterated prisoner's dilemma on a dynamic network, not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implicit (via partner selection) rather than explicit. Thus, while the findings are highly informative for environments with endogenous partner choice, they are only adjacent to explicit punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that, in settings where partner choice is possible, explicit punishment may be less necessary for achieving high efficiency.

- source: Diekmann, A., Jann, B., Przepiorka, W., & Wehrli, S. (2014). Reputation Formation and the Evolution of Cooperation in Anonymous Online Markets. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Using large-scale eBay auction data, the study shows that sellers with more positive feedback (reputation) are more likely to sell their items and receive higher prices, while negative feedback reduces both outcomes. The effect of negative feedback is stronger than that of positive feedback. Participation in the feedback system is high, and feedback behavior is consistent with strong reciprocity and altruism, which helps maintain the reputation system. The reputation system thus mitigates trust problems and supports cooperation in one-shot, anonymous exchanges, increasing overall market efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation-based feedback systems in anonymous, one-shot exchange environments (adjacent to PGGs) increase efficiency by incentivizing cooperative behavior. While not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing or strengthening reputation/feedback mechanisms in similar environments: efficiency (measured as successful trade and higher prices) increases when reputation mechanisms are active and well-participated. The effect is stronger for negative feedback (punishment) than for positive feedback (reward). The study also highlights the importance of high participation in the feedback system, which is sustained by reciprocity and altruism. For prediction, the presence of a reputation/feedback mechanism (even if not formal monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanism.

- source: Rustagi, D., Engel, S., & Kosfeld, M. (2010). Conditional Cooperation and Costly Monitoring Explain Success in Forest Commons Management. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that (1) groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators have significantly better forest management outcomes, (2) costly monitoring (enforcement) is more prevalent in groups with more conditional cooperators, and (3) both conditional cooperation and costly monitoring are important for successful commons management. The main outcome is a real-world efficiency proxy (forest management success), not laboratory earnings. The public goods game is used to measure behavioral types, not as the main outcome. The study does not experimentally manipulate punishment or monitoring, but observes natural variation in monitoring (enforcement) and links it to outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that costly monitoring (enforcement) is associated with higher group-level efficiency in a real-world commons context, and that the presence of conditional cooperators amplifies this effect. While the study does not manipulate punishment in a controlled PGG, it supports the idea that enabling or increasing costly enforcement (monitoring and punishment) can improve group efficiency, especially when conditional cooperators are present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and intensity of enforcement mechanisms (even if costly) are likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, particularly in groups with more conditional cooperators. However, the evidence is observational and context-specific (forest management), not from a lab PGG with toggled punishment.

- source: Wang, R. W., Sun, B. F., Zheng, Q., Shi, L., & Zhu, L. X. (2011). Asymmetric interaction and indeterminate fitness correlation between cooperative partners in the fig-fig wasp mutualism. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric interference competition among cooperative actors (wasps) can stabilize cooperation by keeping resource use unsaturated, leading to positive fitness correlations (mutual benefit) between partners. When interference competition is prevented (sequential entry), or when wasp oviposition efficiency is high (e.g., in cold season), the system can shift to negative fitness correlation (conflict), indicating overexploitation and reduced group efficiency. The relationship between cooperation and efficiency is not fixed but varies stochastically with environmental and experimental factors. The paper also discusses the role of discriminative sanctions (host reducing egg deposition efficiency of less-cooperative wasps) and rewards in maintaining cooperation, but these are not directly manipulated as in PGG punishment/reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms analogous to punishment (sanctions by the host) and interference competition among cooperators can maintain or undermine group efficiency, depending on ecological context and the structure of interaction. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of resource competition, the number of actors, and environmental conditions. However, the system studied is a biological mutualism, not a PGG or direct human social dilemma, and the 'punishment' is not implemented as a discrete, costly action by peers but as host-imposed sanctions. Thus, while the results are adjacent and informative, they should be transferred to PGG predictions with caution, especially regarding the direct effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Cuesta, J. A., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Ferrer, A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2015). Reputation drives cooperative behaviour and network formation in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that providing information about others' past actions (reputation/memory) leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates in a dynamic network Prisoner's Dilemma. When no memory is available, cooperation decays and the network becomes highly connected (as players have no reason to avoid defectors). With memory, players break links with defectors, leading to lower connectivity but higher cooperation. The effect is stronger as the memory window increases. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The 'punishment' mechanism is the breaking of links, not a costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reputation-based partner selection (link breaking) on cooperation in dynamic network games. While the setting is an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (not a PGG), and the 'punishment' is not costly, the results suggest that allowing players to sanction defectors by breaking ties (when reputation is available) can sustain higher cooperation. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not measured, and the game is not a PGG, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that information about others' behavior (reputation) and the ability to avoid defectors can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Xiang, T., Lohrenz, T., & Montague, P. R. (2013). Computational Substrates of Norms and Their Violations during Social Exchange. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that social norm expectations, manipulated via preconditioning to low, medium, or high offers in a repeated ultimatum game, strongly affect rejection rates for identical offers. Subjects preconditioned to low offers (unfair) are less likely to reject medium offers than those preconditioned to high offers (hyper-fair), even though the offers are objectively the same. Subjective feelings about offers and neural responses (in regions such as vmPFC, striatum, and anterior insula) track these norm prediction errors. The results support a model in which norm violations, rather than simple payoff inequality, drive punishment behavior (rejection), and that these processes are reflected in both behavior and brain activity. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, and the outcome of interest is not efficiency but rather the psychological and neural mechanisms of norm-based punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, specifically regarding how expectations and norm adaptation modulate punishment (rejection) behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment (here, costly rejection) on behavior is highly sensitive to context and prior expectations, which may inform models of punishment in other social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as contextual or mechanistic evidence about the psychological drivers of punishment, not as a quantitative source for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are rational maximizers in an ultimatum game. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees, when tested in a mini-ultimatum game, do not systematically reject unfair offers, even when a fair alternative is available to the proposer. Proposers do not make fair offers, and responders accept almost all nonzero offers, only reliably rejecting offers where they receive nothing. This contrasts with human behavior, where unfair offers are often rejected as a form of costly punishment. The findings suggest that chimpanzees are rational maximizers and do not engage in costly punishment to enforce fairness. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses to fairness and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a mini-ultimatum game with chimpanzees and focuses on behavioral outcomes (offer choices, rejection rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative in showing that, in the absence of a willingness to punish unfairness (i.e., when punishment is not exercised), there is no mechanism to enforce fair or cooperative outcomes. This suggests that, in environments where punishment is not used or is not costly, efficiency gains from punishment are unlikely. The findings are adjacent to the prediction task and may inform models about the necessity of actual punishment behavior for efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2002). Crime in the lab-detecting social interaction. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates strong social interaction effects: subjects' willingness to steal increases with the observed or expected stealing of others. About half of the subjects are 'reciprocal' (conditional) types, while the rest are mostly selfish (always steal the maximum). The fraction of selfish types is higher when stealing is a pure transfer (no efficiency loss) than when it is inefficient. The study does not include a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding norm compliance and conditional cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment or on efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about the importance of social interaction and conditional norm violation: when individuals see more norm violation (stealing), they are more likely to violate norms themselves. The manipulation of the efficiency consequences of stealing (gamma) shows that more selfish behavior occurs when norm violation is less costly to the group, which may be relevant for understanding the impact of efficiency parameters in related games. However, because there is no punishment mechanism and no direct efficiency or payoff analysis, this paper should be considered only as indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Ligon, E., & Schechter, L. (2012). Motives for sharing in social networks. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds high baseline sharing in anonymous, random-recipient dictator games, with small but significant increases in sharing when the dictator can choose the recipient or when the dictator's identity is revealed. The decomposition shows that preference-based motives (baseline, directed altruism) account for most mean sharing, but incentive-based motives (sanctions, reciprocity) explain a large share of the variance in sharing and are strongly associated with real-world gift-giving and social network connectedness. The effect of revealing identity (potential for social sanctions or reciprocity) is modest on average but important for explaining individual differences. The study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a set of dictator game variants, not a PGG or linear public goods game, and there is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism—only the possibility of social sanctions or reciprocity inferred from information structure. The findings suggest that the potential for social sanctions or reciprocity can modestly increase sharing, but the context and outcome measures are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency predictions. The results may be useful for understanding how information and recipient choice affect sharing motives, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Seinen, I., & Schram, A. (2006). Social status and group norms: Indirect reciprocity in a repeated helping experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds strong evidence for indirect reciprocity: donors are significantly more likely to help recipients who have helped others in the past, as measured by the recipient's social status. When information about social status is available, cooperation rates are much higher (e.g., 70% in high-cost/information vs. 22% in high-cost/no-information). Individual strategies often use cut-points based on the recipient's or donor's own social status, and group-level norms about the minimum acceptable status to be helped emerge and are stable within groups but differ across groups. The paper does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but does provide some expected return calculations for different strategies. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; 'punishment' is indirect, in the sense that donors withhold help from low-status recipients.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effects of information and social norms on cooperation in repeated helping games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The design does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but the withholding of help from low-status individuals functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the rate of helping (cooperation), not efficiency or group payoff, though some expected returns are calculated. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that information about others' past behavior and the emergence of group norms can sustain high cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the efficiency effects of explicit punishment mechanisms. The design dimensions most relevant here are information availability, cost/benefit structure, and group size, but the absence of explicit punishment and the focus on individual rather than group payoffs limit direct applicability.

- source: West, S. A., Gardner, A., Shuker, D. M., Reynolds, T., Burton-Chellow, M., Sykes, E. M., Guinnee, M. A., & Griffin, A. S. (2006). Cooperation and the scale of competition in humans. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that human participants cooperate more frequently in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games when rewards are based on global competition (top scorers in the whole class) than when rewards are based on local competition (top scorer in each small group). This effect is robust across individuals, groups, and classes. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the scale of competition (local vs global) can strongly affect cooperation rates in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does highlight the importance of competitive structure as a moderator of cooperation. The findings suggest that contextual factors like competition scale may interact with other mechanisms (such as punishment) in affecting cooperation, but no direct evidence is provided for punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Glowacki, L., & von Rueden, C. (2015). Leadership solves collective action problems in small-scale societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in both the Tsimane and Nyangatom societies, leaders tend to have more 'capital' (age, strength, social connections), and that leadership is more likely to become institutionalized in larger, denser, or more conflict-prone communities. Leadership is associated with improved group coordination and conflict mediation, and leaders often incur higher costs (e.g., risk in battle, time spent mediating). However, there is no direct evidence that leaders or leadership increase group efficiency or total payoffs in a way that maps onto public goods game outcomes. The paper notes that leader-based monitoring and sanctioning may reduce free-riding and improve the efficiency of reward or punishment allocation, but this is discussed in general terms and not measured as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for understanding the social and ecological conditions under which leadership, monitoring, and sanctioning emerge to solve collective action problems. It provides indirect support for the idea that leadership (and by extension, centralized punishment or monitoring) can improve group coordination and reduce free-riding, especially as group size and conflict increase. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the efficiency impact of punishment in controlled game environments, this paper offers only contextual and theoretical guidance, not direct empirical input.

- source: Pisor, A. C., Gervais, M. M., Purzycki, B. G., & Ross, C. T. (2020). Preferences and constraints: the value of economic games for studying human behaviour. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johnson, T., McElreath, R., & Smirnov, O. (2007). Egalitarian motives in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that people are willing to pay to reduce or augment others' incomes even when there is no cooperative norm to enforce and no material gain from doing so. Both costly taking (punishment-like) and costly giving (reward-like) are common, and are strongly targeted by relative income: high earners are penalized, low earners are rewarded. Negative emotions towards high earners increase with inequality and predict more costly taking and giving. The design randomizes group composition and income, ensuring that actions cannot be motivated by reciprocity or reputation. The study provides evidence for egalitarian motives in income-altering behavior, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods or cooperation context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a contribution/cooperation stage or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment/reward, but isolates egalitarian motives by removing cooperation. The findings suggest that punishment-like and reward-like behaviors can be motivated by inequality aversion and negative emotions, not just norm enforcement. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological motives behind punishment/reward, not about their effect on efficiency.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Cook, K. S., & Watabe, M. (1998). Uncertainty, trust, and commitment formation in the United States and Japan. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that when social uncertainty is high (i.e., risk of being exploited is greater), participants are more likely to form committed, stable relationships with specific partners, rather than seeking new partners or maximizing short-term gains. This effect is robust across both US and Japanese samples, and is stronger among individuals with lower general trust. The presence or absence of opportunities for exploitation (extortion/appropriation) is manipulated, but the main outcome is the frequency of commitment formation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results support the theory that social uncertainty drives commitment formation, and that general trust 'emancipates' individuals from closed, committed relationships. However, the study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in environments with higher social uncertainty (risk of exploitation), individuals are more likely to form stable, committed relationships, which may facilitate cooperation. However, the experimental design does not include a public goods game, nor does it measure group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome. The 'punishment' or 'sanction' mechanism is limited to opportunities for exploitation (negative actions), not costly punishment of free riders. Therefore, while the findings may inform understanding of how uncertainty and trust affect partner choice and commitment, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The paper is best used as contextual or indirect evidence about the role of uncertainty and trust in cooperation and commitment, rather than as a source of quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Horita, Y., Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., Li, Y., Shinada, M., Miura, A., Inukai, K., Takagishi, H., & Simunovic, D. (2012). Rejection of unfair offers in the ultimatum game is no evidence of strong reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game do not show greater prosociality or positive reciprocity in other economic games (dictator, trust, or prisoner's dilemma). The rejection of unfair offers is not correlated with prosocial value orientation or reciprocity scales, but is positively correlated with assertiveness. The results challenge the interpretation of UG rejection as evidence of strong reciprocity and suggest it may instead reflect a desire to avoid inferior status. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a punishment mechanism analogous to those in PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The study is focused on the ultimatum game, which is structurally different from PGGs, and examines rejection (a form of costly punishment) as a behavioral outcome, not its effect on group efficiency or payoff. The findings suggest that rejection in the UG may not be motivated by the same prosocial or norm-enforcing preferences that drive punishment in PGGs. Therefore, the results caution against interpreting UG rejection as evidence for the effectiveness or motivation of punishment in PGG-like settings, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment.

- source: Halko, M. L., Hlushchuk, Y., Hari, R., & Schürmann, M. (2009). Competing with peers: Mentalizing-related brain activity reflects what is at stake. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Competition in a two-responder ultimatum game increases acceptance rates for medium-sized offers, indicating that people are more likely to accept less-than-equal offers when a competitor is present. This effect is strongest for offers that are not minimal or maximal, and is associated with increased activation in brain regions related to mentalizing and reward. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms. The findings are relevant for understanding how competition changes bargaining behavior, but not for predicting efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is a modified ultimatum game with competition, not a public goods game, and does not include a punishment or sanctioning stage. The main behavioral outcome is acceptance rate, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that competition can shift behavior toward profit maximization, but do not inform how punishment affects efficiency in public goods settings. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency outcomes for punishment-enabled public goods games.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2008). Anticipated verbal feedback induces altruistic behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity for recipients to send anonymous verbal feedback (messages) to dividers in a one-shot dictator game significantly increases the average donation from 24.8% to 34.1% of the endowment. The fraction of zero donations drops from 42% to 21%, and equal splits rise from 30% to 48%. Negative feedback is almost always given when no money is received, and positive feedback is almost always given for equal splits. The results suggest that anticipated symbolic rewards and punishments (praise/blame) can promote altruistic behavior, even in anonymous, one-shot settings. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the design is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that anticipated symbolic sanctions (verbal feedback) can increase prosocial behavior in allocation games, suggesting that non-material punishment/reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation or generosity. However, because the study uses a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated public goods game), does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the 'punishment' is symbolic rather than material, its direct predictive value for efficiency in public goods games with material punishment is limited. The findings may be most relevant for predicting the effect of adding symbolic feedback or communication channels, rather than material punishment, in similar environments.

- source: Oda, R., Niwa, Y., Honma, A., & Hiraishi, K. (2011). An eye-like painting enhances the expectation of a good reputation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of a stylized eye image in a dictator game increases the amount of money allocated to the recipient. This effect is not due to fear of punishment but is mediated by the expectation of a positive reputation or reward, likely from a third party. The study uses a one-shot dictator game with no communication, no punishment or reward mechanisms, and no group-level payoff structure. The findings are relevant to understanding how subtle cues of observation can increase prosocial behavior, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment-enabled environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the amount given by the dictator, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that subtle cues of being watched can increase prosocial behavior via reputation concerns, but do not inform the effect of actual punishment mechanisms or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., Guemo, M., Robertson, T. E., & Tooby, J. (2012). The Psychosemantics of Free Riding: Dissecting the Architecture of a Moral Concept. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that people spontaneously and robustly categorize others as free riders only when they perceive intentional, exploitive undercontribution, not merely low contribution or accidental failure. This categorization is specific and not explained by general tendencies to categorize, by return rate rules, or by a general moral violator rule. Free riders elicit more punitive sentiment and are less preferred as cooperative partners, but these are attitudinal outcomes, not measured payoffs. The study provides evidence for a specialized psychological mechanism for free rider detection, supporting evolutionary game theory predictions about the need to identify and sanction intentional exploiters in collective action. However, the study does not use actual public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment institutions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related economic outcomes. Instead, it provides evidence that people are psychologically prepared to identify and punish intentional free riders, and that this categorization is specific and robust. For the prediction task, this suggests that punishment mechanisms may be more effective at targeting intentional exploiters than accidental undercontributors, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about efficiency effects. Its main value is in informing the psychological underpinnings of punishment and norm enforcement, not in providing payoff-based effect sizes or moderators.

- source: Vonasch, A. J., Reynolds, T., Winegard, B. M., & Baumeister, R. F. (2018). Death Before Dishonor: Incurring Costs to Protect Moral Reputation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that people value their moral reputation extremely highly, often above wealth, success, or even life itself. In hypothetical scenarios, large proportions of participants preferred jail, amputation, or death to acquiring a bad reputation. In lab experiments, many participants chose to endure pain or disgust (cold pressor, touching worms) rather than have information suggesting they were racist shared with others. The motivation to avoid reputation damage is strong and can drive costly behavior. However, the paper does not use public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a game-theoretic sense, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, support the broader claim that reputation concerns can motivate costly prosocial or norm-compliant behavior, which is a mechanism often invoked in explaining why punishment can sustain cooperation. The findings may be useful for contextualizing why individuals might comply with norms or avoid free-riding, but they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effects of specific game design dimensions. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant and should not be weighted heavily for estimating efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wallace, B., Cesarini, D., Lichtenstein, P., & Johannesson, M. (2007). Heritability of ultimatum game responder behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in the willingness to reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game are substantially heritable, with additive genetic effects explaining about 42% of the variance in acceptance thresholds. The study uses a large sample of monozygotic and dizygotic twins from the Swedish Twin Registry and applies standard structural equation modeling. There is no evidence that common environment plays a significant role. The results are robust across sexes. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment institutions, and does not use a public goods game or a repeated game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to showing that individual differences in costly punishment behavior (as measured by ultimatum game rejections) have a substantial genetic component. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not use a public goods or repeated game design. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are vengeful but not spiteful. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Chimpanzees do not engage in 'spiteful' punishment (i.e., incurring a cost to reduce another's payoff in response to disadvantageous outcomes alone), but do engage in 'vengeful' punishment (i.e., retaliating when a conspecific actively steals food from them). The tendency to retaliate declines over repeated sessions, and is not affected by kinship or prior reciprocity. There is no evidence that such punishment-like behavior enforces cooperation or increases group welfare, as stealing increases over time and retaliation declines. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides evidence about the motivations underlying punishment-like behaviors in chimpanzees.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not use a PGG or measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. However, it is informative for understanding the motivational basis of punishment: chimpanzees retaliate against harmful actions but do not punish mere outcome inequality. This suggests that, in species or contexts where punishment is only vengeful and not spiteful, punishment may not stabilize cooperation or improve efficiency as it does in human PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the underlying motivation and context (e.g., response to intentional harm vs. outcome disparity) matter. However, the lack of payoff outcomes and the non-PGG design limit its direct predictive value.

- source: Jin, S. X., Balliet, D., Romano, A., Spadaro, G., van Lissa, C. J., Agostini, M., Bélanger, J. J., Gützkow, B., Kreienkamp, J., & Leander, N. P. (2021). Intergenerational conflicts of interest and prosocial behavior during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that age is associated with perceived costs of the pandemic (older people perceive higher health risks, younger people perceive more daily life disruption and job insecurity), but age is not robustly associated with prosocial COVID-19 behaviors (such as social distancing or staying at home) or support for behavioral regulations (such as mandatory quarantine or vaccination). The study conceptualizes pandemic-related prosocial behaviors as a social dilemma but does not use a public goods game or direct analog, nor does it measure or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. No experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions is present; support for behavioral regulations is measured as an attitude, not as an implemented or experienced sanctioning institution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses social dilemmas and support for behavioral regulations (as a proxy for second-order cooperation), it does not manipulate or implement punishment, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The findings are based on self-reported attitudes and behaviors in a real-world pandemic context, not on controlled game-theoretic experiments. Therefore, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited and indirect at best.

- source: Basu, S., Dickhaut, J., Hecht, G., Towry, K., & Waymire, G. (2009). Recordkeeping alters economic history by promoting reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing subjects to keep records in a repeated multi-dyad trust game leads to higher mean investor returns (18.7% vs. 7.3% in periods 6-10) and lower risk (coefficient of variation 0.221 vs. 0.358) compared to no-recordkeeping conditions. Recordkeeping promotes reputation formation, more coordinated reciprocal behavior, and greater discrimination between trustworthy and untrustworthy partners. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that external memory (recordkeeping) can beneficially alter economic history by increasing efficiency and reducing risk in complex exchange environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that informational institutions like recordkeeping can substantially increase efficiency and reduce risk in repeated trust-based exchange, especially in complex (multi-partner) settings. While it does not study punishment, it demonstrates that mechanisms which improve memory and reputation tracking can have large positive effects on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling information storage or reputation tracking (even without explicit punishment) can boost efficiency, particularly as the number of partners increases and over repeated rounds. The results are most directly relevant to settings where the main challenge is memory and reputation, not norm enforcement via punishment.

- source: Knoch, D., Schneider, F., Schunk, D., Hohmann, M., & Fehr, E. (2009). Disrupting the prefrontal cortex diminishes the human ability to build a good reputation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when trustees know their past actions will be visible to future partners (reputation condition), they return significantly more to investors than in the anonymous condition. Disrupting the right DLPFC with rTMS eliminates this effect, causing trustees to behave as if reputation incentives are absent. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is highest (i.e., when the investor sends the maximum amount). The study provides causal evidence that the right DLPFC is necessary for overcoming short-term temptations to build a good reputation. However, the paper does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not the primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only weakly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is a repeated trust game with reputation incentives, not a public goods game, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main behavioral outcome is back-transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative about the role of reputation and self-control in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about punishment effects or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Francey, D., & Bergmüller, R. (2012). Images of Eyes Enhance Investments in a Real-Life Public Good. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Images of eyes, as subtle cues of being watched, increased the amount of time and effort people invested in removing garbage from public bus stop benches, but did not affect the likelihood that people would engage in garbage removal at all. The effect was not due to increased attention to a sign requesting garbage removal. The study provides field evidence that reputational cues can enhance non-monetary investments in public goods, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is as indirect evidence that reputational cues (such as being watched) can increase cooperative investment in real-world public good contexts, but this is not mediated by punishment or efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the prediction task, but may provide weak contextual support for the importance of reputational mechanisms.

- source: Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., & Yamagishi, T. (2010). Altruism toward in-group members as a reputation mechanism. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
