  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of eye images (cues of monitoring) on participants' computer screens increased the frequency of fair (equal) allocations in a one-shot dictator game, but only when the recipient was an in-group member. No such effect was found for out-group recipients. The effect is interpreted as evidence that reputation concerns (triggered by monitoring cues) selectively enhance altruism toward in-group members. The study does not involve repeated interaction, public goods, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcome is allocation behavior rather than group payoff or efficiency. The only relevant dimension is the effect of monitoring cues (eyes) on prosocial behavior, which may be loosely analogous to reputation or observability manipulations in public goods games, but not to punishment. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Bradley, A., Lawrence, C., & Ferguson, E. (2018). Does observability affect prosociality?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that making prosocial actions observable increases prosocial behavior (r = 0.141), with stronger effects in repeated interactions, social dilemmas (including public goods games), and when decisions have consequences for the actor. The effect is also stronger in laboratory settings and with passive observers. However, the outcome is always a behavioral measure (e.g., contribution, donation, volunteering) or intention, not efficiency or group payoff. The analysis does not directly address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observability (e.g., making contributions public) can increase prosocial behavior in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in repeated games and social dilemmas. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or the effect of punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that increasing observability may increase contributions, which could in turn affect efficiency, but without direct evidence. It does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report the relevant game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct mapping to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Blair, G., Littman, R., & Paluck, E. L. (2019). Motivating the adoption of new community-minded behaviors: An empirical test in Nigeria. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study found that both a social norms intervention (film with role models) and a nudge intervention (mass text message) increased the number of people who reported corruption by text message in Nigerian communities. The mass text message had a much larger and more immediate effect than the film. The film shifted some perceptions of social norms (e.g., increased perceived prevalence of corruption and anger about corruption), but did not increase the perceived prevalence of reporting. There was no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and no punishment or sanction mechanism was present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental context is adjacent to public goods (encouraging costly community-minded behavior), but there is no group payoff, no efficiency measure, and no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding the adoption of rare cooperative behaviors, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Ernest-Jones, M., Nettle, D., & Bateson, M. (2011). Effects of eye images on everyday cooperative behavior: a field experiment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Displaying images of eyes in a cafeteria significantly reduced the likelihood of littering (i.e., increased norm compliance/cooperation), independent of whether the accompanying message was relevant to littering. The effect was strongest when there were fewer people present (i.e., when natural cues of observation were sparse). The study provides evidence that subtle cues of being watched can promote cooperative behavior in real-world settings, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how cues of observation (such as eye images) can increase norm compliance in real-world settings, especially when actual observation is low. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, marginal per-capita return, or other core PGG design dimensions, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of observation cues on cooperation, but not for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bracht, J., & Feltovich, N. (2009). Whatever you say, your reputation precedes you: Observation and cheap talk in the trust game. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares four treatments: (1) baseline trust game, (2) trust game with cheap talk (allocator sends a nonbinding message), (3) trust game with observation (investor sees allocator's previous action), and (4) both cheap talk and observation. Observation of previous actions leads to a significant and substantial increase in both trust (investment) and trustworthiness (splitting), resulting in higher aggregate efficiency. Cheap talk, implemented as a binary, anonymous message, has little or no effect on behavior or efficiency. The effect of observation disappears in the final round, consistent with reputation incentives. The results suggest that even minimal, accurate information about past actions can improve efficiency in social dilemmas without coercion or costly punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-coercive observation mechanisms (reputation via observed past actions) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even in the absence of punishment or rewards. However, the game is a two-player trust game, not a public goods game, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs is limited. The findings suggest that, in environments where players can observe others' past cooperative behavior, efficiency is likely to be higher than in baseline games without such information, even without punishment. The paper does not study punishment, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that information mechanisms alone can have large positive effects. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of observation or reputation mechanisms (even without punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, and that the effect of cheap talk depends strongly on its implementation.

- source: Efferson, C., Lalive, R., & Fehr, E. (2008). The coevolution of cultural groups and ingroup favoritism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated two-player coordination game with two equilibria, allowing players to freely choose and maintain symbolic markers (shapes) leads to the endogenous formation of 'cultural groups' where markers become predictive of behavior. This results in strong ingroup favoritism (preference for same-marker partners), increased coordination on the optimal behavior, and significantly higher mean payoffs compared to a control where markers are randomized and cannot acquire meaning. When the payoff structure removes unobservable heterogeneity or markers are fixed, the effect disappears. The findings show that endogenous group formation via symbolic markers can substantially increase efficiency in coordination games, but only under specific conditions (marker mutability and persistent heterogeneity). No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in coordination games with multiple equilibria and persistent unobservable heterogeneity, allowing players to endogenously form groups via flexible symbolic markers can increase efficiency (mean payoff) by enabling better coordination. However, the design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results are most relevant for understanding how group markers and endogenous assortment affect efficiency, not punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight the importance of group structure and endogenous assortment for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Grujic, J., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Traulsen, A., Cuesta, J. A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2014). A comparative analysis of spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments: Conditional cooperation and payoff irrelevance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments on lattices, cooperation rates start high but quickly decline to low, nonzero levels (15-35%). (2) Defectors consistently earn more than cooperators, with a significant negative correlation between cooperation and earnings. (3) The behavioral rule most consistent with the data is moody conditional cooperation, where players' probability to cooperate depends on their own previous action and the number of cooperating neighbors. (4) There is no evidence that spatial structure (network topology, number of neighbors, or system size) promotes higher cooperation or changes the behavioral rule. (5) Payoff-based imitation rules (imitate-the-best, Fermi rule) do not explain the observed behavior. (6) The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions, and does not report group efficiency relative to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates and group payoffs remain low in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games, and that spatial structure alone does not increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper may help establish a baseline for control (no-punishment) efficiency in spatial PD-like environments, but offers no information about the effect of enabling punishment. The behavioral findings (moody conditional cooperation) may inform models of baseline behavior in the absence of incentives.

- source: Nettle, D., Colléony, A., & Cockerill, M. (2011). Variation in Cooperative Behaviour within a Single City. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds dramatic differences in cooperative behavior between two neighborhoods within the same city, with the deprived neighborhood showing much lower generosity in the Dictator Game, lower social capital, and less willingness to engage in certain forms of anonymous helping. However, face-to-face helping rates did not differ. The Dictator Game allocations correlated with social capital and with some real-world cooperative behaviors at the community level. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Dictator Game (a unilateral allocation task) rather than a multi-person social dilemma, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings are relevant to understanding variation in prosociality and cooperation across communities, but not to the prediction task focused on punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Uncalculating cooperation is used to signal trustworthiness. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that uncalculating cooperation is used as a signal of trustworthiness: participants are more likely to cooperate without calculating costs when their decision process is observable to others, and such behavior leads to greater trust and actual trustworthiness in a subsequent trust game. The findings support a reputation-based account of uncalculating cooperation. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in public goods or similar group cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about reputation and uncalculating cooperation may be of theoretical interest for understanding motivations for cooperation, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Blake, P. R., Rand, D. G., Tingley, D., & Warneken, F. (2015). The shadow of the future promotes cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma for children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Children aged 10-11 cooperate significantly more in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games than in one-shot games, demonstrating sensitivity to the 'shadow of the future' and the potential for direct reciprocity. Conditional cooperation strategies are observed, with girls and children with fewer conduct problems being more forgiving and cooperative. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how repeated interaction (direct reciprocity) increases cooperation in dyadic games among children, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task, mainly by illustrating that repeated interaction alone (without punishment) can increase cooperation rates in dyadic settings.

- source: Fehr, E., & Leibbrandt, A. (2011). A field study on cooperativeness and impatience in the Tragedy of the Commons. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individual differences in laboratory-measured cooperativeness and patience predict real-world resource conservation behavior among fishermen. Specifically, higher contributions in a public goods game and greater patience in a time preference task are associated with using fishing gear that is less harmful to the resource (larger holes in shrimp traps, larger mesh sizes in fishnets). The link is robust across two samples (shrimpers and fishermen) and persists after controlling for various covariates. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The main outcomes are behavioral proxies for resource conservation, not direct payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or CPR settings. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that laboratory measures of cooperativeness and patience predict field behavior in resource exploitation, but it does not inform how punishment or game design dimensions affect efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Harrison, F., Sciberras, J., & James, R. (2011). Strength of Social Tie Predicts Cooperative Investment in a Human Social Network. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative investment (measured as willingness to endure physical discomfort for another's benefit) is positively correlated with the strength of the social tie between donor and recipient, and is higher when the relationship is reciprocated. Participants sometimes invested more for close peers than for themselves. The study does not involve a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze group-level efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant to understanding how social proximity and reciprocity affect individual-level cooperative behavior, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency/payoff. Its findings about social tie strength and reciprocity may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Capraro, V., & Cococcioni, G. (2015). Social setting, intuition and experience in laboratory experiments interact to shape cooperative decision-making. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, among Indian participants (a population presumed to have internalized non-cooperative strategies), promoting intuition (via time pressure) versus deliberation (via time delay) does not affect cooperation rates among inexperienced subjects. However, experienced subjects (those with prior exposure to economic games) are more cooperative than inexperienced ones, but only under time pressure. The main dependent variable is the percentage of endowment transferred in a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a two-person continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma without punishment or reward options. The findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, group payoffs) are low in non-cooperative settings without institutional support or experience, and that experience with economic games can increase cooperation under intuitive conditions. However, since no punishment mechanism is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation levels in non-cooperative settings.

- source: Romano, A., Balliet, D., Yamagishi, T., & Liu, J. H. (2017). Parochial trust and cooperation across 17 societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people cooperate more with ingroup than outgroup members in a trust game, but reputational concern (common knowledge of group membership) increases cooperation with both ingroup and outgroup members. Men show slightly more ingroup favoritism than women, but social value orientation predicts general cooperation rather than parochialism. There is little cross-societal variation in ingroup favoritism, and this variation is not explained by material security, religiosity, or pathogen stress. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how reputational concerns (a form of indirect social incentive) can increase cooperation, but not for payoff-based or punishment-based interventions. Its design and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, offering only indirect context for how social information and group identity affect cooperation.

- source: Dunning, D., Anderson, J. E., Schlösser, T., Ehlebracht, D., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2014). Trust at Zero Acquaintance: More a Matter of Respect Than Expectation of Reward. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that trust in zero-acquaintance trust games is driven more by internalized moral norms (especially a norm of respect) than by expectations of reward or risk tolerance. Participants report that they 'should' trust more than they 'want' to, and this sense of obligation predicts trust behavior. Making trust decisions public does not increase trust, suggesting the norm is moral rather than social. When the trust game is altered so that the recipient's character is not at stake (e.g., outcome determined by coin flip), trust rates drop. The paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, and thus does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that in trust games without punishment, trust rates can be high due to internalized moral norms, even when efficiency or payoff maximization would not predict such behavior. The findings are only indirectly relevant to public goods games, and not at all relevant to punishment or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2010). People recognise when they are really anonymous in an economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, proposers' offers are sensitive to whether their decisions are public or anonymous, but not to the mere presence of others if anonymity is maintained. The presence of known others increases offers in public conditions but decreases them in anonymous-in-public conditions. Responders' minimum acceptable offers are not affected by anonymity or social context. The findings suggest that explicit information about anonymity can override social cues, and that reputation concerns drive higher offers when decisions are public. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and is not directly about public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies the effect of anonymity and social context on cooperative behavior in the ultimatum game, but does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social context and reputation concerns affect cooperative choices, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., Lergetporer, P., & Sutter, M. (2016). Cooperation and discrimination within and across language borders: Evidence from children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that children cooperate more with in-group members (same class), less with out-group members of the same language, and least with out-group members of a different language. Both in-group favoritism and language group discrimination are observed, with the latter emerging mainly among older German-speaking children. Cooperation increases with age, and girls cooperate more than boys. Beliefs about partner cooperation are higher for in-group partners and are positively correlated with own cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or reward mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. However, it provides contextual evidence on baseline cooperation rates and discrimination in the absence of punishment, which may be relevant for understanding control conditions in similar environments. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Insko, C. A., Kirchner, J. L., Pinter, B., Efaw, J., & Wildschut, T. (2005). Interindividual-intergroup discontinuity as a function of trust and categorization: The paradox of expected cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that groups are less cooperative and more competitive than individuals in a one-shot, three-choice PDG-Alt game. Manipulating trust (high vs. low) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both groups and individuals, but does not significantly affect competition. Manipulating categorization (same vs. different) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both, but increases competition only for groups, especially among those high in guilt proneness. The main dependent variables are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how group context, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how group structure, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition in a PDG-variant. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline group vs. individual differences in cooperation in the absence of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral choices rather than payoffs limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

- source: Lyle, H. F., & Smith, E. A. (2014). The reputational and social network benefits of prosociality in an Andean community. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of punishment or differential allocation of collective goods, individuals who contribute more to collective action gain reputational benefits and larger social support networks. These networks are associated with improved health outcomes for households. The results support the idea that reputational and network benefits can help sustain cooperation in collective action settings, even when free riding is not punished. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or surplus, and does not use a public goods game or experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a real-world collective action setting without punishment or sanctions, reputational and social network benefits can sustain cooperation. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or payoff outcomes as defined in public goods games, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is as a case study of how cooperation can be maintained without punishment, via reputational and network mechanisms.

- source: Northover, S. B., Pedersen, W. C., Cohen, A. B., & Andrews, P. W. (2017). Artificial surveillance cues do not increase generosity: two meta-analyses. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The meta-analyses find that artificial surveillance cues do not significantly increase generosity, measured either as the amount given or the probability of giving. The effect sizes are very small and not significantly different from zero. The studies included cover a range of economic games and donation paradigms, but the dependent variable is always generosity, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency-related outcomes. The findings suggest that artificial surveillance cues should not be expected to increase prosocial giving in anonymous settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The meta-analyses focus on the effect of artificial surveillance cues on generosity, not on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. While one included study uses a public goods game, the outcome is individual generosity, not group efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Grujic, J., Eke, B., Cabrales, A., Cuesta, J. A., & Sánchez, A. (2012). Three is a crowd in iterated prisoner's dilemmas: experimental evidence on reciprocal behavior. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in iterated multiplayer Prisoner's Dilemma games, cooperation is sustained and even increases in two-player groups, but decays rapidly and remains low in groups of three or more. The main behavioral mechanism is 'moody conditional cooperation,' where players' likelihood to cooperate depends on both their own previous action and the number of cooperators in the previous round. There is substantial heterogeneity in initial generosity but less in reciprocity. The presence of more than two players leads to a sharp drop in sustained cooperation, suggesting that reciprocity alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation in larger groups without additional mechanisms such as punishment. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, increasing group size from 2 to 3 or more sharply reduces sustained cooperation in iterated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, it suggests that, without punishment, efficiency (if measured) would be high in two-player games and low in groups of three or more. However, because the study does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, it cannot directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of group size as a moderator of cooperation and suggests that additional mechanisms (like punishment) may be necessary to sustain cooperation and efficiency in larger groups.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Smyth, C., Mylona, K., & Niblo, G. A. (2014). Benevolent Characteristics Promote Cooperative Behaviour among Humans. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) benevolence—defined as unilaterally increasing another's payoff beyond one's own—exists in the population, (2) individuals who are more benevolent are more likely to cooperate in a subsequent one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, and (3) priming participants to think benevolently increases their cooperation rate compared to priming malevolence. The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Romano, A., Sutter, M., Liu, J. H., Yamagishi, T., & Balliet, D. (2021). National parochialism is ubiquitous across 42 nations around the world. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people across 42 nations cooperate more with ingroup (same-nation) partners than with outgroup or unidentified partners in a one-shot, continuous prisoner's dilemma. This national parochialism is highly consistent across nations and is not explained by cultural distance, institutional quality, or ecological factors. Observability (public vs private) increases overall cooperation but does not moderate parochialism. There is substantial cross-national variation in baseline cooperation rates (impersonal cooperation), which is associated with cultural and ecological factors. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design (continuous PD, no punishment, no repeated interaction) is adjacent to but not directly informative for the downstream prediction task. The only potentially relevant dimension is the effect of observability (public vs private) on cooperation, but this is not tied to efficiency or payoff, and there is no punishment treatment.

- source: Gächter, S., Gerhards, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2017). The importance of peers for compliance with norms of fair sharing. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of peers strongly influences what participants perceive as socially appropriate sharing, but this does not translate into corresponding changes in actual sharing behavior. There is substantial heterogeneity in both norm perceptions and willingness to comply with norms. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how peer information shapes norm perceptions and sharing behavior in dictator-like settings, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Haley, K. J., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2005). Nobody's watching? Subtle cues affect generosity in an anonymous economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that subtle visual cues of being watched (eyespots) significantly increase generosity in a one-shot dictator game, as measured by both the mean amount allocated and the proportion of dictators who give any money. The effect of reducing auditory cues (earmuffs) is small and not statistically significant. The results suggest that even in anonymous, noniterated economic games, intuitive or affective cues of observability can increase prosocial behavior. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Its findings about subtle cues of observability increasing generosity in dictator games may be contextually interesting for understanding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Cesarini, D., Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johannesson, M., Lichtenstein, P., & Wallace, B. (2008). Heritability of cooperative behavior in the trust game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in trust and trustworthiness in the trust game are partially explained by genetic variation, with heritability estimates ranging from 10-20% for trust and 17-18% for trustworthiness. The majority of behavioral variance is due to unshared environmental factors. There is little evidence that common environment (e.g., family upbringing) explains much of the variance. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game studied (trust game) is only adjacent to public goods games. The findings are only relevant for understanding the sources of individual variation in cooperative behavior, not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Epstein, Z. G. (2014). Risking Your Life without a Second Thought: Intuitive Decision-Making and Extreme Altruism. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who engage in extreme altruism overwhelmingly describe their decision-making as intuitive, fast, and automatic, rather than deliberative or reasoned. This is supported both by human ratings of interview statements and by linguistic analysis of inhibitory language. The results are robust even in cases where the altruist had time to deliberate. The study does not involve any economic games, public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions of interest (e.g., player count, punishment, MPCR, etc.), nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about intuitive decision-making in real-world extreme altruism may be of theoretical interest for understanding the psychology of cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., Martin, J. W., Treadway, M. T., Jan, K., Zald, D. H., Jones, O., & Marois, R. (2015). From Blame to Punishment: Disrupting Prefrontal Cortex Activity Reveals Norm Enforcement Mechanisms. *Neuron*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) plays a selective, causal role in punishment decision-making for norm violations. Disrupting DLPFC function via rTMS reduces the amount of punishment assigned to culpable agents, especially for low- to moderate-harm crimes, but does not affect blameworthiness ratings. fMRI results show that DLPFC activity is higher during punishment decisions than during blameworthiness judgments. The study supports an integration-and-selection model in which DLPFC integrates information about harm and culpability to determine punishment. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any economic game with payoffs, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use such games or report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms of punishment assignment in norm enforcement, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency or group payoff in economic games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context about punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Falk, A., Becker, A., Dohmen, T., Enke, B., Huffman, D., & Sunde, U. (2018). GLOBAL EVIDENCE ON ECONOMIC PREFERENCES. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds substantial heterogeneity in economic preferences across and within countries. Preferences such as patience, risk tolerance, altruism, reciprocity, and trust are systematically related to individual characteristics (age, gender, cognitive ability) and to country-level geographic and cultural variables. Patience is strongly correlated with economic development (GDP per capita), while negative reciprocity is correlated with the frequency of armed conflicts. Social preferences (altruism, positive reciprocity, trust) are associated with prosocial behaviors and charitable activities. However, the study does not involve public goods games, group efficiency, or experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it measures negative reciprocity (willingness to punish unfairness) as a preference and relates it to macro-level outcomes (e.g., conflict), it does not analyze the effect of punishment opportunities on group payoff, efficiency, or cooperation in controlled game settings. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very indirect context on the prevalence of punishment motives across societies.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2009). Experimental Games and Games of Life among the Ju/'hoan Bushmen. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The Ju/'hoan Bushmen made low offers and rarely punished in anonymous dictator and ultimatum games, behaving more selfishly than in their daily lives, where sharing and social sanctions are common. The anonymity and lack of social context in the experimental games removed the cultural institutions that normally support generosity and punishment. In daily life, sharing is widespread and norm violations are sanctioned, usually through verbal criticism and exclusion rather than costly punishment. The study highlights the importance of cultural context and institutions for the expression of other-regarding behavior and social sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a public goods game or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the importance of social context and cultural institutions for cooperation and punishment, but do not inform quantitative predictions about punishment's effect on efficiency in experimental economic games with or without punishment. The study suggests that the presence or absence of social context and identification may strongly moderate the willingness to punish or cooperate, but provides no direct evidence for the target prediction task.

- source: Mazar, N., Amir, O., & Ariely, D. (2008). The Dishonesty of Honest People: A Theory of Self-Concept Maintenance. *Journal of Marketing Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across six lab experiments, the authors find that (1) people cheat when given the opportunity, but only by a limited amount; (2) reminders of moral standards (e.g., Ten Commandments, honor code) eliminate or reduce cheating; (3) increasing categorization malleability (e.g., using tokens instead of money) increases cheating; (4) cheating is not sensitive to the probability of being caught or the magnitude of external rewards within the tested range; (5) cheating does not affect self-reported self-concept as honest/moral; and (6) beliefs about others' cheating do not affect individual cheating. The results support a psychological model of dishonesty based on self-concept maintenance, not external punishment or cost-benefit analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or public goods outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctions as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding psychological mechanisms of norm compliance and dishonesty, and may be indirectly informative for the design of interventions (e.g., moral reminders) to reduce cheating, but do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Gould, R. V. (2000). Revenge as sanction and solidarity display: An analysis of vendettas in nineteenth-century Corsica. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that only about 10-15% of violent attacks were followed by revenge, and that such retaliation was most likely when the original attack involved multiple assailants (collaboration) or targeted nondisputants (kin uninvolved in the dispute). Revenge was not indiscriminately directed at all kin of the offender, but rather at those who had demonstrated solidarity with the original offender, either by collaborating in the attack or by aiding the offender afterward. The structure of revenge mirrored the kin relationships involved in the original offense, suggesting a strategic, solidarity-signaling function. The findings challenge the idea of automatic collective responsibility and instead support a model of 'contingent collectivism,' where group-level retaliation is conditional on group-level aggression.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about the social logic and structure of revenge in a real-world, stateless society context, not about experimental games or efficiency. However, it may offer indirect, contextual insight into the conditions under which group-level sanctions (punishment) are likely to be deployed: namely, when group-level (collaborative) violations occur. For the prediction task, this suggests that the presence of group-level aggression or collaboration may be a moderator of the likelihood or intensity of punishment, but the paper does not address efficiency or payoff consequences.

- source: Kasper, C., & Mulder, M. B. (2015). Who Helps and Why? Cooperative Networks in Mpimbwe. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in a rural Tanzanian village, helping behavior is generally low and is best predicted by reciprocity (contingency), with kinship and affinal relationships also playing roles. Wealthier individuals are less likely to participate in reciprocal helping relationships and receive less help. Geographic proximity and age also influence helping patterns. The study discusses the erosion of traditional cooperation networks in the context of increasing market integration and inequality. Witchcraft accusations are mentioned as a form of social sanction, but there is no formal punishment system or experimental manipulation of sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or any formal game, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The study is observational, not experimental, and focuses on naturalistic helping behavior, not on the effects of introducing or varying punishment mechanisms. Witchcraft accusations are discussed as a form of social pressure, but not in a way that is structurally or functionally equivalent to PGG punishment treatments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some adjacent context about the role of social sanctions in real-world cooperation, but not in a way that is mappable to the target prediction problem.

- source: Exadaktylos, F., Espín, A. M., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2013). Experimental subjects are not different. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both student status and self-selection (volunteering) can have small effects on social preference behaviors, but these effects are generally minor and not economically significant. Self-selected students (the typical lab subject pool) behave very similarly to the broader population in Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. The only significant differences are small and mainly concern strategic behavior and trust/reciprocity among non-students. The results support the external validity of using self-selected students for social preference experiments, but do not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any public goods game, punishment, or sanctioning treatment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are only relevant to the representativeness of subject pools in social preference experiments using Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Sutter, M., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Lergetporer, P. (2018). Language group differences in time preferences: Evidence from primary school children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that German-speaking children are significantly more patient (more likely to delay gratification) than Italian-speaking children, even after controlling for age, IQ, risk attitudes, and family background. This difference is robust across different elicitation methods and is also reflected in self-reported saving behavior. Children from bilingual households show intermediate levels of patience. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not study group cooperation, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on time preferences and cultural/language group differences in patience are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public goods games.
